The addition of temporal neighborhood makes the logic of prefixes and sub-intervals EXPSPACE-complete \lsuper *
Laura Bozzelli ORCID:0000-0003-0963-8169, Angelo Montanari ORCID:0000-0002-4322- 769X, Adriano Peron ORCID:0000-0002-7111-3171, Pietro Sala ORCID:0000-0002-2612-1519

Abstract
. A classic result by Stockmeyer [ Sto74 ] gives a non-elementary lower bound to the emptiness problem for generalized ∗ * -free regular expressions. This result is intimately connected to the satisfiability problem for the interval temporal logic of the chop modality under the homogeneity assumption [ HMM83 ] . The chop modality can indeed be viewed as the inverse of the concatenation operator of regular languages, and such a correspondence enables reductions between the two problems. In this paper, we study the complexity of the satisfiability problem for suitable weakenings of the chop interval temporal logic, that can be equivalently viewed as fragments of Halpern and Shoham interval logic. We first introduce the logic 𝖡𝖣 h ​ o ​ m \mathsf{BD}_{hom} featuring modalities B B (for begins ), corresponding to the prefix relation on pairs of intervals, and D D (for during ), corresponding to the infix relation, whose satisfiability problem, under the homogeneity assumption, has been recently shown to be PSpace-complete [ BMPS21b ] . The homogeneous models of 𝖡𝖣 h ​ o ​ m \mathsf{BD}_{hom} naturally correspond to languages defined by restricted forms of generalized ∗ * -free regular expressions, that feature operators for union, complementation, and the inverses of the prefix and infix relations. Then, we study the extension of 𝖡𝖣 h ​ o ​ m \mathsf{BD}_{hom} with the temporal neighborhood modality A A , corresponding to the Allen relation Meets , and prove that such an addition increases both the expressiveness and the complexity of the logic. In particular, we show that the resulting logic 𝖡𝖣𝖠 h ​ o ​ m \mathsf{BDA}_{hom} is ExpSpace-complete .

1.
Introduction

Interval temporal logics (ITLs for short) are versatile and expressive formalisms to specify properties of sequences of states and their duration. When it comes to fundamental problems like satisfiability, their high expressive power is often obtained at the price of undecidability. As an example, the satisfiability problem of the most widely known ITLs, namely, Halpern and Shoham’s
\mathsf{HS}

[
HS91
]
and Venema’s
\mathsf{CDT}

[
Ven91a
]
, turn out to be highly undecidable. Despite these negative results, a number of decidable ITLs have been identified by suitably weakening
\mathsf{HS}
(see
[
BMM+14
]
for a complete classification of
\mathsf{HS}
fragments).
Here the term “weakening” is intended as a set of syntactic and/or semantics restrictions imposed on the formulas of the logic
and/or the temporal structures on which such formulas are interpreted, respectively.
Among the plethora of possible weakenings, here
we focus on (the combination of) the following two natural and well-studied restrictions:

•

Restrict the set of interval relations
.

A number of decidable fragments of
\mathsf{HS}
and
\mathsf{CDT}
have been obtained by considering a restricted set of Allen’s relations over pairs of intervals. This approach naturally induces fragments of
\mathsf{HS}
with modalities corresponding to the selected subset of interval relations. As an example, the logic of temporal neighborhood (
\mathsf{PNL}
for short) features only two modalities, corresponding to two interval relations among the thirteen possible ones, namely,
A
(
adjacent to the right
) and its inverse
\bar{A}
(
adjacent to the left
)
[
CH97
]
.
\mathsf{PNL}
has been shown to be decidable over all meaningful classes of linear orders
[
BMSS11
,
MS12
]
.

•

Restrict the class of models
.

As an alternative, it is possible to tame the complexity of ITLs by restricting to classes of models that satisfy some specific assumptions. An example of such an approach can be found in a recent series of papers that study the model checking problem for ITLs (see, e.g., the seminal paper
[
MMM+16
]
),
as well as their expressiveness compared to that of classical point-based temporal logics, like
\mathsf{LTL}
,
\mathsf{CTL}
, and
\mathsf{CTL^{*}}

[
BMM+19a
]
.
In this setting, models are represented as Kripke structures, and are inherently point-based rather than interval-based. The very same models can be obtained from interval temporal structures by making the so-called
homogeneity assumption
, i.e., by assuming that every proposition letter holds over an interval if and only if it holds at all its points
[
Roe80
]
. Under such an assumption, full
\mathsf{HS}
has a decidable satisfiability problem (as a matter of fact, the model checking procedures proposed in the aforementioned series of papers can be easily turned into satisfiability procedures, often retaining the same complexity)
[
MMM+16
]
. In the light of the above, the focus in studying
\mathsf{HS}
fragments under the homogeneity assumption was shifted from decidability to complexity.

Under the homogeneity assumption, a natural connection to generalized
*
-free regular languages emerges from the analysis of the complexity of ITLs over finite linear orders.
A classic result by Stockmeyer states that the emptiness problem for generalized
*
-free
regular expressions is non-elementarily decidable (tower-complete)
for unbounded nesting of negation
[
Sch16
,
Sto74
]

(it is
(K-1)-ExpSpace-complete
for expressions where the nesting
of negation is at most
\textsc{K}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
).
Such a problem can be easily turned into the satisfiability problem for the logic
\mathsf{C}
of the chop modality, over finite linear orders, under the homogeneity assumption
[
HMS08
,
Mos83
,
Ven91b
]
, and vice versa.
\mathsf{C}
is a proper fragment of
\mathsf{CDT}
with a single binary modality, the so-called
chop
operator, that allows one to split the current interval in two parts and to state what is true over the first part and what over the second one. It can be easily shown that there is a reduction, that operates in logarithmic space, of the emptiness problem for generalized
*
-free regular expressions to the satisfiability problem for
\mathsf{C}
with unbounded nesting of the chop operator, and vice versa.

The close relationships between formal languages and ITLs have been already pointed out in
[
MS13a
,
MS13b
,
DMS23
]
, where the ITL counterparts of regular languages,
\omega
-regular languages, and extensions of them (
\omega B
-,
\omega S
-, and
\omega T
-regular languages) have been provided. Here, we focus on some meaningful fragments of
\mathsf{C}
under the homogeneity assumption. Hereafter, for any ITL
\mathsf{X}
, we write
\mathsf{X}_{hom}
to point out that we are considering
\mathsf{X}
under the homogemeity assumption. Modalities for the prefix, suffix, and infix relations over (finite) intervals can be easily defined in
\mathsf{C}
. It holds that a formula holds over a prefix of the current interval if and only if it is possible to split the interval in such a way that the formula holds over the first part and the second part contains at least two points. The case of suffixes is completely symmetric. Infixes can be defined in terms of prefixes and suffixes: a proper sub-interval of the current interval is a suffix of one of its prefixes or, equivalently, a prefix of one of its suffixes.

The satisfiability problem for the logic
\mathsf{D}_{hom}
of the infix relation has been shown to be
PSpace-complete
by a suitable contraction method
[
BMM+17
,
BMM+22
]
.
PSpace
completeness has been recently proved also for the logic
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
(resp.,
\mathsf{DE}_{hom}
) that extends
\mathsf{D}_{hom}
with modality
B
(resp.,
E
) for the prefix (resp., suffix) relation
[
BMPS21b
,
BMPS23
]
.

A lot of work has been done on the logic
\mathsf{BE}_{hom}
of prefixes and suffixes.
In
[
BMM+19b
]
,
its satisfiability problem has been shown to be
ExpSpace-hard
by a polynomial-time reduction from a domino-tiling problem for grids with rows of single exponential length. Moreover, all the other
\mathsf{HS}_{hom}
fragments whose satisfiability problem is known to be
ExpSpace-hard
feature
\mathsf{BE}_{hom}
as a proper fragment. As for the upper bound, a trivial one is given by the non-elementary decision procedure for full
\mathsf{HS}_{hom}
devised in
[
MMM+16
]
(
\mathsf{BE}_{hom}
is a small fragment of
\mathsf{HS}_{hom}
). In
[
BMP19
]
, Bozzelli et al. showed that it is not possible to improve such a bound by tailoring the proof techniques exploited for
\mathsf{HS}_{hom}
in
[
MMM+16
]
, which are based on the notion of
\mathsf{BE}
-descriptor, to
\mathsf{BE}_{hom}
, as it is not possible to give an elementary upper bound on the size of
\mathsf{BE}
-descriptors for
\mathsf{BE}_{hom}

[
BMP19
]
.
ExpSpace
membership (from which
ExpSpace
completeness immediately follows) of the satisfiability problem for
\mathsf{BE}_{hom}
has been very recently shown by devising an equi-satisfiable normal form with boundedly many nested modalities
[
DMPS23
]
. The normalization technique somehow resembles Scott’s quantifier elimination, but it turns out to be much more involved due to the limitations enforced by the homogeneity assumption.

In this paper, we focus on the logic
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
, that extends
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
with the
meet
modality
\mathsf{A}
, and prove that its satisfiability problem is
ExpSpace-complete
by a model-theoretic argument. As a matter of fact,
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
turns out to be the first
ExpSpace-complete
fragment of
\mathsf{HS}_{hom}
that does not feature
\mathsf{BE}_{hom}
as a proper fragment.
As a preparatory step, we apply the proposed model-theoretic proof technique to the simpler fragment
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
, and then we show how to extend it to
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
without any increase in complexity.

The paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we provide a gentle introduction to ITLs. We first give an informal account of the two main propositional ITLs, namely
\mathsf{CDT}
and
\mathsf{HS}
, interpreted over finite linear orders. Then, by making use of a simple example, we compare their expressive power with that of Linear Temporal Logic (
\mathsf{LTL}
). In Section
3
, we introduce the logic

\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
. We specify its syntax and semantics, and we point out some interesting connections between its formulas and restricted forms of generalized
*
-free regular expressions. In the following three sections, we prove a small model theorem for the satisfiability of
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formulas over finite linear orders, which provides a doubly exponential bound (in the size of the formula) on their models, and exploit it to devise a satisfiability checking procedure that works in exponential space with respect to the size of the input
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formula. More precisely, in Section
4
, we introduce and discuss a spatial representation of the models of
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formulas, called
compass structure
. Then, in Section
5
, we prove a series of meaningful spatial properties of these structures.
Finally, in Section
6
,
we prove a small model theorem for
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
, that allows us to prove the
ExpSpace
membership of the problem of checking the satisfiability of
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formulas over finite linear orders
1
1

1

It is worth noticing that, in view of the results in
[
BMPS21b
]
, where
PSpace
membership is shown, the proposed decision procedure is sub-optimal. However, it plays a fundamental instrumental role in the proof of the main result of the paper about
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
.

In Section
7
, we define the logic
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
, that extends
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
with modality
\mathsf{A}
; then, we show that the properties stated for
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
in Section
5
holds for
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
as well, and, building on them, we prove the
ExpSpace
membership of the problem of checking the satisfiability of
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
formulas over finite linear orders.
The decision procedure for
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
can be obtained from that for
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
by making a few small adjustments.
ExpSpace-hardness
of the satisfiability problem for
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
, over finite linear orders, is proved in Section
8
, by a reduction from the acceptance problem for (non-deterministic) Turing Machines working in exponential space, thus allowing
us to conclude that the complexity bound for
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
finite satisfiability is tight.
Section
9
concludes the paper with an assessment of the work done and an outline of future research directions. Supplementary material, including additional examples and some of the most technical proofs, that will be referenced throughout the paper, is provided in the appendices. In particular, proofs are reported in Appendix
B
.

2.
A gentle introduction to Interval Temporal Logics

In this section, we provide a gentle introduction to Interval Temporal Logics (ITLs), focusing on the features that distinguish them from point-based ones. As a natural term of comparison, we choose
\mathsf{LTL}
, and,
for the sake of simplicity, we restrict our attention to totally ordered finite temporal structures, that is, finite prefixes of
\mathbb{N}

(finite
traces
or models). With a little abuse of notation, we denote the ordered set
\{0,1,2,\ldots,N\}
by
N
.

\mathsf{LTL}
over finite traces is often referred to as
\mathsf{LTL}_{f}
in the literature
[
GV13
,
GMM14
]
.

Let
\mathrm{Prop}
be a set of proposition letters. The first, crucial difference between ITLs and
\mathsf{LTL}_{f}
is the way in which
\mathrm{Prop}
is interpreted over models. Let
\mathbb{I}_{N}=\{[x,y]:0\leq x\leq y\leq N\}
be the set of all and only the intervals on
N
. In the case of
\mathsf{LTL}_{f}
, the valuation function is
\pi:N\rightarrow 2^{\mathrm{Prop}}
, while, in the case of ITLs, it is
\mathcal{V}:\mathbb{I}_{N}\rightarrow 2^{\mathrm{Prop}}
. It is immediate to see that
\mathcal{V}
is a generalization of
\pi
, as the point-based semantics
\pi
can be embedded into the interval-based one
\mathcal{V}
by assuming
\pi(x)=\mathcal{V}([x,x])
, for all
x\in N
. From now on, we will refer to intervals of the forms
[x,x]
and

[x,y]
, with
x<y
, as
point-intervals
and
strict-intervals
, respectively. Whenever we will not need to distinguish between point- and strict-intervals, we will simply refer to them as
intervals
.

\begin{array}[]{c||c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|c}\mathcal{V}([x,y])&[0,0]&[0,1]&[0,2]&[0,3]&[0,4]&[1,1]&[1,2]&[1,3]&[1,4]&[2,2]&[2,3]&[2,4]&[3,3]&[3,4]&[4,4]\\

Figure 1.
Point-based (
\pi
) vs. interval-based (
\mathcal{V}
) labelling over the same finite linear order.

In its full generality, ITL interval-based semantics does not impose any constraint on the relationships between the proposition letters that hold over a strict-interval and those that hold over the point-intervals that it includes, i.e., the set of proposition letters
\mathcal{V}([x,y])
that hold over the strict-interval
[x,y]
may differ from the sets of proposition letters
\mathcal{V}([x,x]),\ldots,\mathcal{V}([y,y])
that hold on the point-intervals
in

[x,y]
(which, obviously, may differ from each other). Similarly, the set of proposition letters
\mathcal{V}([x^{\prime},y^{\prime}])

that hold on a proper subinterval
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
of
[x,y]
, that is,
x\leq x^{\prime}<y^{\prime}\leq y
and
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]\neq[x,y]
, may differ from those that hold on
[x,y]
. Consider the example of Figure
1
, where
\pi
and
\mathcal{V}
agree on the labelling of points
0,\ldots,4
(they are interpreted as the intervals
[0,0],\ldots,[4,4]
in the ITL semantics). The evaluation of proposition letters
p
and
q
on strict-intervals does not depend on that on their sub-intervals.
As an example, the label of the interval
[1,4]
in Figure
1
is
\mathcal{V}([1,4])=\{p,q\}
and it features all the possible subsets of
\{p,q\}
as the labels of its point intervals
[1,1],\ldots[4,4]
. As for its proper subintervals, it holds that

\mathcal{V}([1,2])=\mathcal{V}([2,3])=\mathcal{V}([3,4])=\emptyset
,
\mathcal{V}([1,3])=\{p\}
,
and
\mathcal{V}([2,4])=\{p,q\}
.

The semantics of the three
\mathsf{CDT}

,

, and

.

One of the first ITLs proposed in literature was
\mathsf{CDT}

[
Ven90
]
, whose name comes from its three binary modalities
\ \mathbf{C}\

(
Chopping
),
\ \mathbf{D}\
(
Dawning
), and
\ \mathbf{T}\

(
Terminating
). Their semantics is graphically depicted in Figure
2
. Intuitively speaking, if we take a point
z

inside an interval
[x,y]
and we consider the ternary relation

[x,y]

may be split into

[x,z]

and

[z,y]
,
the three
\mathsf{CDT}
modalities allow one to talk about
the properties of such a relation starting from any of the three
intervals. More precisely, a formula
\psi_{1}\ \mathbf{C}\ \psi_{2}
(
chopping between
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
) holds over an
interval
[x,y]
if
[x,y]
can be split into
[x,z]
and
[z,y]
,
\psi_{1}
holds over
[x,z]
, and
\psi_{2}
holds over
[z,y]
(topmost part of Figure
2
).
A formula
\psi_{1}\ \mathbf{D}\ \psi_{2}
(
\psi_{1}
dawning
\psi_{2}
) holds over an interval
[x,y]
if
there exists an interval
[z,x]
such that
\psi_{1}
holds over
[z,x]

and
\psi_{2}
holds over the interval
[z,y]
covering both
[z,x]
and
[x,y]
(middle part of
Figure
2
). A formula
\psi_{1}\ \mathbf{T}\ \psi_{2}
(
\psi_{1}
terminating
\psi_{2}
) holds
over an interval
[x,y]
if there exists an interval
[y,z]
such that

\psi_{1}
holds over
[y,z]
and
\psi_{2}
holds over the interval
[x,z]

covering both
[x,y]
and
[y,z]

(bottom part of Figure
2
).

\mathsf{CDT}
turns out to be very expressive. It can be easily checked that it allows one to specify a number of meaningful properties in
a rather straightforward way. As an example, it is easy to write a

\mathsf{CDT}
formula that forces one or more proposition
letters to behave like an equivalence relation over the points of
the underlying linear order. However, such an expressivity
is paid with an undecidable satisfiability problem on every
interesting linear order, that is, any linear order but finite, bounded ones,
where the problem is trivially decidable. The same undecidability results hold for any of the
\mathsf{CDT}
fragments that feature just one modality among
\ \mathbf{C}\ \!
,
\ \mathbf{D}\ \!
, and
\ \mathbf{T}\

[
GMSS06
,
HMS08
]
.

A representative fragment of
\mathsf{CDT}
is
\mathsf{HS}

[
HS91
]
, which includes a unary modality for each ordering relation between a pair of intervals (the so-called Allen’s relations
[
All81
]
). A graphical account of Allen’s relations and the corresponding
\mathsf{HS}
modalities is given in Figure
3
.
For the sake of simplicity, we deliberately omitted the modality for the inverse of each of the depicted relations, namely
\langle\overline{A}\rangle,\langle\overline{B}\rangle,\langle\overline{D}\rangle,\langle\overline{E}\rangle,\langle\overline{L}\rangle
, and
\langle\overline{O}\rangle
.
The semantics of each
\mathsf{HS}
modality can be expressed by means of a suitable combination of
\mathsf{CDT}
modalities as shown in
Appendix
A
.
The converse is not true. In Figure
17
, we make an extensive use of the modal
constant
\pi
, which holds over an interval
[x,y]
if and only if

x=y
, that is,
[x,y]
is a point-interval.
It immediately follows that
\neg\pi
holds on all and only strict intervals.
It is worth pointing out that some
\mathsf{HS}
modalities can be defined as suitable combinations of other ones (a complete account of definability equations for the most
significant classes of linear orders is given in
[
BMM+14
,
BMM+19c
]
). For what concerns the
\mathsf{HS}
fragments we focus on in this paper, namely those featuring unary modalities
\langle A\rangle,\langle B\rangle
, and
\langle D\rangle
(not to be confused with the binary modality
\ \mathbf{D}\
of
\mathsf{CDT}
), it holds that modality
\langle L\rangle
can be defined in terms of modality
\langle A\rangle
and modality
\langle D\rangle
can be expressed as a suitable combination of modalities
\langle B\rangle
and
\langle E\rangle
. Notice that the opposite is not true, e.g., modality
\langle A\rangle
cannot be defined in terms of modalities
\langle L\rangle,\langle D\rangle
, and
\langle B\rangle
and it is not possible to define modality
\langle B\rangle
(resp.,
\langle D\rangle
) in terms of modalities
\langle A\rangle
and
\langle D\rangle
(resp.,
\langle B\rangle
).

relation
\mathsf{HS}
operator

Example

\mathrm{OVERLAPS}

\langle E\rangle
\langle L\rangle
\langle O\rangle
[x,y]\ \scalebox{0.5}{$\mathrm{MEETS}$}\ [v,z]\Leftrightarrow y=v

Allen’s relations and the corresponding

We conclude the section by showing that
\mathsf{LTL}_{f}
modalities
Until
(
\psi_{1}\ \mathrm{U}\ \psi_{2}
) and
Next
(
\varbigcirc\psi
) can be easily encoded by means of a combination of modalities
\langle A\rangle
and
\langle B\rangle
(no need to bring up modality
\langle D\rangle
).
Formulas that define
\psi_{1}\ \mathrm{U}\ \psi_{2}
and
\varbigcirc\psi
in
\mathsf{AB}
, together with a graphical account of how they “operate” on an interval model, are given in Figure
4
.
The
\mathsf{LTL}_{f}
formula into which
\psi_{1}\ \mathrm{U}\ \psi_{2}
is mapped is the conjunction of formulas
[B]\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi_{1})
and
\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi_{2})
. By definition,
\psi_{1}\ \mathrm{U}\ \psi_{2}
holds at a point
x
if there is a point
y
, with
x\leq y
, where
\psi_{2}
holds and
\psi_{1}
holds at
x_{i}
, for all
x\leq x_{i}<y
.
The proposed encoding forces the translation of
\psi_{1}\ \mathrm{U}\ \psi_{2}
to hold over the interval
[x,y]
. The second conjunct of the resulting formula constrains
\psi_{2}
to hold on
[y,y]
(the right endpoint of
[x,y]
). The formula
\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi_{2})
indeed states that there is an interval
[y,y^{\prime}]
, that starts where
[x,y]
ends (modality
\langle A\rangle
), which is a point-interval (constant
\pi
) and satisfies
\psi_{2}
. It immediately follows that
[y,y^{\prime}]
is equal to
[y,y]
. The first conjunct
[B]\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi_{1})
forces the formula
\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi_{1})
to hold on each proper prefix (
[B]=\neg\langle B\rangle\neg
) of the interval
[x,y]
, that is, on each interval
[x,x_{i}]
, with
x\leq x_{i}<y
. Then, by the argument we already used for
\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi_{2})
, we can conclude that
\psi_{1}
holds at each point-interval
[x_{i},x_{i}]
, with
x\leq x_{i}<y
.
As for modality
\varbigcirc
, the
\mathsf{LTL}_{f}
formula
\varbigcirc\psi
is mapped into the
\mathsf{AB}
formula
\langle A\rangle(\neg\pi\wedge[B]\pi\wedge\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi))
. By definition,
\varbigcirc\psi
holds at a point
x
if and only if
\psi
holds at the point
x+1
. For the sake of generality and simplicity, the proposed encoding of
\varbigcirc\psi
holds on an interval
[x,y]
if and only if
\psi
holds at the point-interval
[y+1,y+1]
regardless of the fact that
[x,y]
is a strict- or a point-interval (
[x,y]
can be forced to be a point-intervals by adding
\pi
as a conjunct of the resulting formula, i.e.,
\pi\wedge\langle A\rangle(\neg\pi\wedge[B]\pi\wedge\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi))
). Whenever
\langle A\rangle(\neg\pi\wedge[B]\pi\wedge\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi))
holds over an interval
[x,y]
, the outermost modality
\langle A\rangle
imposes
the existence of an interval
[y,y^{\prime}]
, with
y\leq y^{\prime}
, where
\neg\pi
,
[B]\pi
, and
\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi)
hold. The first two conjuncts respectively force
y^{\prime}>y
(
\neg\pi
) and all proper prefixes
[y,y^{\prime\prime}]
of
[y,y^{\prime}]
to be point-intervals (
[B]\pi
). The only way to satisfy them is to constrain
y^{\prime}
to be equal to
y+1
.
The truth of
\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi)
on

[y,y+1]
implies that there is an interval
[y+1,y^{\prime}]
where both
\pi
and
\psi
hold, which amounts to say that
\psi
holds over the point-interval
[y+1,y+1]
.

It is worth pointing out that the truth values of proposition letters on strict-intervals do not come into play in the proposed encoding. It immediately follows that such an encoding still properly works under the homogeneity assumption.

\psi_{1}\ \mathrm{U}\ \psi_{2}=[B]\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi_{1})\wedge\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi_{2})

)

)

\langle A\rangle(
\neg\pi\wedge[B]\pi\wedge\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi)
)

)

Figure 4.
The encoding of
\mathrm{LTL}_{f}
modalities

.

In the appendix (see Figure

),
we show how to exploit such an encoding to translate formulas
p\ \mathrm{U}\ (\neg p\wedge\neg q)
and
\varbigcirc(\neg p\wedge\neg q)
)
into equivalent
\mathsf{AB}
formulas and, by means of the example of Figure

,
we show how the interval model is constrained
when the resulting formula holds over an interval.

3.
The logic
\mathsf{BD}
of prefixes and infixes

In this section, we introduce the logic
\mathsf{BD}
of prefixes and infixes,
we formally state the homogeneity assumption, and we define the relation of finite satisfiability
under such an assumption. We conclude the section with a short analysis
of the relationships between such a logic and a suitable restriction of
generalized
*
-free regular expressions.

\mathsf{BD}
formulas are built up from a countable set
\mathrm{Prop}
of proposition letters according to the grammar:

\varphi::=p\ |\ \neg\psi\ |\ \psi\vee\psi\ |\ \langle B\rangle\psi\ |\ \langle D\rangle\psi,

where
p\in\mathrm{Prop}
and
\langle B\rangle
and
\langle D\rangle
are the modalities for Allen’s relations
Begins
and
During
, respectively.
In the following, we denote by
|\varphi|
the size of the parse tree
for a formula
\varphi
generated by
the above grammar. It is easy to show that

|\varphi|
is less than or equal to the number of symbols used to encode
\varphi
.

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
be a natural number and let
\mathbb{I}_{N}
be the set of all intervals over the prefix
0\ldots N
of
\mathbb{N}
. A (finite) model for
\mathsf{BD}
formulas is
a pair
\mathbf{M}=(\mathbb{I}_{N},\mathcal{V})
, where
\mathcal{V}:\mathbb{I}_{N}\rightarrow 2^{\mathrm{Prop}}
is a valuation that maps intervals in
\mathbb{I}_{N}
to sets of
proposition letters.
Given a model
\mathbf{M}
and an interval
[x,y]
, the semantics of a
\mathsf{BD}
formula is defined as follows:

•

\mathbf{M},[x,y]\models p
iff
p\in\mathcal{V}([x,y])
;

•

\mathbf{M},[x,y]\models\neg\psi
iff

\mathbf{M},[x,y]\not\models\psi
;

•

\mathbf{M},[x,y]\models\psi_{1}\wedge\psi_{2}
iff

\mathbf{M},[x,y]\models\psi_{1}
and
\mathbf{M},[x,y]\models\psi_{2}
;

•

\mathbf{M},[x,y]\models\langle B\rangle\psi
iff
there is
y^{\prime}
, with
x\leq y^{\prime}<y
, such that

\mathbf{M},[x,y^{\prime}]\models\psi
;

•

\mathbf{M},[x,y]\models\langle D\rangle\psi
iff
there exist
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}
, with
x<x^{\prime}\leq y^{\prime}<y
, such that
\mathbf{M},[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]\models\psi
.

The logical constants
\top
(true) and
\bot
(false), the Boolean connectives

\vee,\rightarrow
, and
\leftrightarrow
, and the (universal) dual modalities

[B]
and
[D]
are defined in the usual way.
The modal constant
\pi
, that has been introduced in Section
2
, is defined as
[B]\bot
.

(a)

0

\{p,r\}

1

\{p\}

2

\{p,q,r\}

\{p,q,r\}

4

\{p,q,r\}

5

\{q,r\}

6

\{q\}

7

\{q\}

\{p\}

\{p,q,r\}

\{q\}

\begin{array}[]{c|c}[x,y]&\mathcal{V}_{a}(x,y)\\

\end{array}

Figure 5.

A homogeneous model (a - left) vs. a general one (b - right).

We say that a
\mathsf{BD}
formula
\varphi
is
finitely satisfiable
if and only if there exist
a (finite) model
\mathbf{M}
and an interval
[x,y]
such that
\mathbf{M},[x,y]\models\varphi
W.l.o.g.,
[x,y]
can be assumed to be the maximal interval
[0,N]
.
Hereafter, whenever we use the term
satisfiable
, we always mean finite satisfiability, that is, satisfiability over the class of finite linear orders.

{defi}

[Homogeneity]
We say that a model
\mathbf{M}=(\mathbb{I}_{N},\mathcal{V})

satisfies the homogeneity property
(
\mathbf{M}
is
homogeneous
for short) if and only if

\forall p\in\mathrm{Prop}~~\forall[x,y]\in\mathbb{I}_{N}~~\Big(p\in\mathcal{V}([x,y])~\Leftrightarrow~\forall z\in[x,y]~p\in\mathcal{V}([z,z])\Big).

{exa}

In Figure
5
,

we give an example of a homogeneous model (a) and of an arbitrary non-homogeneous one (b). For the sake of readability, we will refer to them as

\mathbf{M}_{a}=(\mathbb{I}_{7},\mathcal{V}_{a})
and
\mathbf{M}_{b}=(\mathbb{I}_{7},\mathcal{V}_{b})
, respectively.
The complete definitions of
\mathcal{V}_{a}
and
\mathcal{V}_{b}

are given in Figure
5
below the respective models. It is easy to check that
the definition of
\mathcal{V}_{a}
satisfies the homogeneity property as stated by Definition
3
.

To begin with, we observe that, in homogeneous models,
the labelling
\mathcal{V}
of the intersection of two intervals contains the labellings of the two intervals. This is the case, e.g., with intervals
[1,4]
and
[2,6]
in Figure
5
(a), whose intersection is the interval
[2,4]
. This is not the case with arbitrary models. Consider, e.g., the very same intervals in Fig.
5
(b). Interval
[1,4]
violates the homogeneity property because
r\in\mathcal{V}_{b}(1,4)

but
r\notin\mathcal{V}_{b}(1,1)
, thus violating the left-to-right direction of
Definition
3
. Interval
[2,4]

violates the homogeneity property as well, because

q\in\mathcal{V}_{b}(2,2)\cap\mathcal{V}_{b}(3,3)\cap\mathcal{V}_{b}(4,4)
,
but
q\notin\mathcal{V}_{b}(2,4)
(the same for
r
), thus violating the right-to-left direction of
Definition
3
.
All the other intervals in Figure
5
(b), including
[2,6]
, satisfy the homogeneity property, but this is obviously not sufficient to consider the model homogeneous,
since
each interval
of the model
must
satisfy it.

It is worth pointing out that the homogeneity property
does not
entail, in general, a similar containment property for formulas
\psi\notin\mathrm{Prop}
.
As an example, it is easy to check that in the homogeneus model of Figure
5
(a)
\mathbf{M}_{a},[1,4]\models\langle B\rangle(p\wedge\neg q)
, but
\mathbf{M}_{a},[2,4]\nmodels\langle B\rangle(p\wedge\neg q)
, and
\mathbf{M}_{a},[2,6]\models\langle D\rangle(q\wedge\neg r)
, but
\mathbf{M}_{a},[2,4]\nmodels\langle D\rangle(q\wedge\neg r)
.

To conclude, notice that, in homogeneous models, for proposition letters it holds that the labelling of point-intervals determines that of all intervals.
This is not the case with arbitrary models. Counterexamples are intervals
[1,4]
and
[2,4]
in Figure
5
(b).

Satisfiability can be relativized to homogeneous models as follows.
We say that a
\mathsf{BD}
formula
\varphi
is
satisfiable under homogeneity
if there
is a homogeneous model
\mathbf{M}

such that
\mathbf{M},[0,N]\models\varphi
. Satisfiability under homogeneity is clearly more restrictive than plain satisfiability.
We know from
[
MM14
,
MMK10
]
that dropping the homogeneity
assumption makes
\mathsf{D}
undecidable. This is not the case with the fragment
\mathsf{B}
, whose expressive power is quite limited, which remains decidable
[
GMS04
]
.
Hereafter, we will always refer to
\mathsf{BD}
under the homogeneity assumption, denoted by
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
.

We conclude the section by giving a short account of the relationships between
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}

and generalized
*
-free regular expressions. Let
\Sigma
be a finite alphabet.
A
generalized
*
-free regular expression
(hereafter, simply
expression
)

e
over
\Sigma
is a term of the form:

e::=\emptyset\ |\ a\ |\ \neg e\ |\ e+e\ |\ \ e\cdot e,\mbox{ for any $a\in\Sigma$.}

We exclude the empty word
\epsilon
from the syntax, as it makes the correspondence between finite words and finite models of
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formulas easier (such a simplification is quite common in the literature). An expression
e
defines a language
\mathrm{Lang}(e)\subseteq\Sigma^{+}
, which is inductively defined as follows:

•

\mathrm{Lang}(\emptyset)=\emptyset
;

•

\mathrm{Lang}(a)=\{a\}
, for every
a\in\Sigma
;

•

\mathrm{Lang}(\neg e)=\Sigma^{+}\setminus\mathrm{Lang}(e)
;

•

\mathrm{Lang}(e_{1}+e_{2})=\mathrm{Lang}(e_{1})\cup\mathrm{Lang}(e_{2})
;

•

\mathrm{Lang}(e_{1}\cdot e_{2})=\{w_{1}w_{2}:w_{1}\in\mathrm{Lang}(e_{1}),w_{2}\in\mathrm{Lang}(e_{2})\}
.

In
[
Sto74
]
, Stockmeyer shows that the problem of deciding the emptiness of
\mathrm{Lang}(e)
, for a given expression
e
, is non-elementary hard.

Let us consider now the logic
\mathsf{C}
of the chop operator (under the homogeneity assumption), that we introduced in Section
2
.

\mathsf{C}
features the chop modality
\ \mathbf{C}\
and the modal constant
\pi
.
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
is a small fragment of
\mathsf{C}
, whose modalities
\langle B\rangle
and
\langle D\rangle
can be defined in
\mathsf{C}
as follows (see Appendix
A
for details):
\langle B\rangle\psi=\psi\ \mathbf{C}\ \neg\pi
and
\langle D\rangle\psi=\neg\pi\ \mathbf{C}\ (\psi\ \mathbf{C}\ \neg\pi)
.

It can be shown that, for any expression
e
over
\Sigma
, there exists a formula
\varphi_{e}
of
\mathsf{C}
whose set of models is the language
\mathrm{Lang}(e)
, that is,
\mathrm{Lang}(e)=\{\mathcal{V}(0,0)\ldots\mathcal{V}(N,N):(\mathbb{I}_{N},\mathcal{V})\models\psi_{e}\}
. Such a formula is the conjunction of two sub-formulas
\psi_{\Sigma}
and
\psi_{e}
.
\psi_{\Sigma}
guarantees that
each point interval
[x,x]
of the model is labelled by exactly one proposition letter from
\Sigma
;
\psi_{e}
constrains the valuation on the basis of the inductive structure of (the translation of)
e
. As an example, if
e=e_{1}\cdot e_{2}
, then
\psi_{e}=\psi_{e_{1}}\ \mathbf{C}\ ((\neg\pi\wedge\neg(\neg\pi\ \mathbf{C}\ \neg\pi))\ \mathbf{C}\ \psi_{e_{2}})
.

Such a mapping of expressions in
\mathsf{C}
formulas allows one to conclude that the satisfiability problem for
\mathsf{C}
is non-elementary hard (its non-elementary decidability follows from the opposite mapping). A careful look at the expression-to-formula mapping reveals that modality
\mathsf{C}
only comes into play in the translation of expressions featuring the operator of concatenation. In view of that, it is worth looking for subclasses of generalized
*
-free regular expressions where the concatenation operation is used in a very restricted manner, so as to avoid the need of modality
\mathsf{C}
in the translation.

Let us focus our attention on the following class of
restricted expressions
:

e::=\emptyset\ |\ a\ |\ \neg e\ |\ e+e\ |\ \ \mathrm{Pre}(e)\ |\ \mathrm{Inf}(e),\mbox{ for any $a\in\Sigma$},

where
\mathrm{Pre}(e)
and
\mathrm{Inf}(e)
are respectively a shorthand for
e\cdot(\neg\emptyset)
(thus defining the language
\mathrm{Lang}(\mathrm{Pre}(e))=\{wv:w\in\mathrm{Lang}(e),v\in\Sigma^{+}\}
), and
(\neg\emptyset)\cdot e\cdot(\neg\emptyset)
(thus defining the language
\mathrm{Lang}(\mathrm{Inf}(e))=\{uwv:u,v\in\Sigma^{+},w\in\mathrm{Lang}(e)\}
).
Every restricted expression
e
of the above form can be mapped into an equivalent formula
\varphi_{e}
of
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
by applying the usual constructions for empty language, letters, negation, and union, plus the following two rules: (i)
\varphi_{\mathrm{Pre}(e)}=\langle B\rangle\psi_{e}
, and (ii)
\varphi_{\mathrm{Inf}(e)}=\langle D\rangle\psi_{e}
.

In the following sections,
we will show that the satisfiability problem for
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
belongs to
ExpSpace
. From the above mapping, it immediately follows that the emptiness problem for the considered subclass of expressions, that only uses prefixes and infixes, can be decided in exponential space (rather than in non-elementary time).

4.
Homogeneous compass structures

In this section, we introduce a spatial representation of homogeneous models,
called
homogeneous compass structures
, that will considerably simplify
the proofs of the next sections.

Let
\varphi
be a
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formula. We define the
closure
of

\varphi
, denoted by
\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)
, as the set of all its sub-formulas and of their negations,
plus formulas
\langle B\rangle\top
and
[B]\bot
. For every

\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formula
\varphi
, it holds that
\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\leq 2|\varphi|+2
.

{defi}

A
\varphi
-atom
(
atom
for short) is a maximal subset
F
of
\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)
that,
for all
\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)
, satisfies the following two conditions
(as usual, we identify every formula of the form
\neg\neg\psi
as
\psi
):

\psi\in F
if and only if
\neg\psi\notin F
(consistency), and

if
\psi=\psi_{1}\vee\psi_{2}
, then

\psi\in F
if and only if
\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\}\cap F\neq\emptyset
(completeness).

Let
\mathrm{At}(\varphi)
be the set of all

\varphi
-atoms. We have that
|\mathrm{At}(\varphi)|\leq 2^{\frac{|\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)|}{2}}
, and, from

\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\leq 2|\varphi|+2
, we have

|\mathrm{At}(\varphi)|\leq 2^{|\varphi|+1}
.

It is easy to see that, given a model
\mathbf{M}=(\mathbb{I}_{N},\mathcal{V})
,
we can uniquely associate an atom
F^{[x,y]}
in
\mathrm{At}(\varphi)

with each interval
[x,y]\in\mathbb{I}_{N}
by simply letting

F^{[x,y]}=\{\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi):\mathbf{M},[x,y]\models\psi\}
.
An example of such an extension of the labelling
\mathcal{V}
to atoms is given
in Figure
6
both in a graphical (top)
and in a tabular (bottom) form.

A distinctive feature of atoms is that any atom
F
is uniquely determined by its subset of formulas

(F\cap\mathrm{Prop})\cup(F\cap\{\langle B\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\})\cup(F\cap\{\langle D\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\})
, that is, once we establish which proposition letters,
\langle B\rangle
sub-formulas, and
\langle D\rangle
sub-formulas belong to it, we can automatically derive all the other formulas
\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)
belonging to it. This subset of its formulas can be viewed as the
fingerprint
of the atom.
In Figure
6
,
we exploit such a feature to identify the atoms associated with the various intervals (we simply specify the formulas in
(F\cap\mathrm{Prop})\cup(F\cap\{\langle R\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\})
belonging to them – boxes in the middle of intervals).

Figure 6.

A graphical (top) and tabular (bottom) account of
\mathrm{Req}_{R}(F)
and
\mathrm{Obs}_{R}(F)
, with
F\in\mathrm{At}(\varphi)
and
R\in\{B,D\}
, for the formula
\varphi=\langle B\rangle(p\wedge\neg r)\wedge\langle D\rangle(\neg q\wedge\langle D\rangle q)
.

The next definition introduces three
functions that characterize the
temporal behavior
of atoms.

{defi}

Let
\varphi
be a
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formula and let
R\in\{B,D\}
.
We associate with each atom
F\in\mathrm{At}(\varphi)
three subsets of
\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)
defined as follows:

•

\mathrm{Req}_{R}(F)=\{\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi):\langle R\rangle\psi\in F\}
(temporal requests);

•

\mathrm{Obs}_{R}(F)=\{\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi):\langle R\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\wedge\psi\in F\}
(observables);

•

\mathrm{Box}_{R}(F)=\{\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi):[R]\psi\in F\}
(universal conditions).

Let us now give a short account of the roles of the sets
\mathrm{Req}_{R}(F)
,
\mathrm{Obs}_{R}(F)
, and
\mathrm{Box}_{R}(F)
, and of their relationships. To start with, we observe that if an atom
F
associated with an interval
[x,y]
contains a formula
\langle R\rangle\psi

(that is,
\langle R\rangle\psi
is true at
[x,y]
),
then there must be another interval
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]

such that
[x,y]
and
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
satisfy relation
R
and
\psi
belongs to the atom
F^{\prime}
associated with
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
(i.e.,
\psi
is true at

[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
).
According to Definition
4
, this amounts to say that
\psi\in\mathrm{Req}_{R}(F)
and
\psi\in\mathrm{Obs}_{R}(F^{\prime})
.
Notice that, for each atom
F
, if
\psi\in\mathrm{Obs}_{R}(F)
, then
\psi\in F
as well, that is,
\mathrm{Obs}_{R}(F)\subseteq F
. In other words,
\mathrm{Obs}_{R}(F)
allows us to “mark” the formulas in (the atom associated with) an interval which can possibly be used to satisfy some
\langle R\rangle
-requests of another interval. It is worth pointing out that

\psi\in\mathrm{Obs}_{R}(F)
and
\psi\notin\mathrm{Req}_{R}(F)
, i.e.,
\langle R\rangle\psi\notin F
, is a perfectly legitimate configuration for an atom, where
\psi
holds on the corresponding interval
[x,y]
, but not on any interval which is reachable from
[x,y]
via relation
R
. In such a case, according to
Definition
4
(consistency) and Definition
4
, we have that
\neg\psi\in\mathrm{Box}_{R}(F)
, i.e.,
\neg\psi
belongs to every atom
F^{\prime}
associated with an interval
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
such that
[x,y]
and
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
satisfy relation
R
, and thus, again by consistency of atoms,

\psi\notin\mathrm{Obs}_{R}(F^{\prime})
.
Last but not least, we observe that for each
F\in\mathrm{At}(\varphi)
and
\psi\in\{\psi^{\prime}:\langle B\rangle\psi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\}
,
by consistency and completeness of atoms, either
\psi\in\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)
or
\neg\psi\in\mathrm{Box}_{B}(F)
. Hence,
\mathrm{Box}_{B}(\cdot)
is not strictly necessary; we introduced it to simplify some proofs. The same holds with
\psi\in\{\psi^{\prime}:\langle D\rangle\psi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\}
.

Let
[x,y]
be an interval. We denote the atom associated with
[x,y]
by
F^{[x,y]}
.
In Figure
6
(top), for each interval
[x,y]
, we report the values of
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})
and

\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[x,y]})
to the right of the box containing the fingerprint of
F^{[x,y]}
; moreover, we add the formula
\varphi
if (and only if) it holds on
[x,y]
.
In Figure
6
(bottom), we give a tabular account of the whole atom, e.g., the atom
F^{[1,2]}
associated with the interval
[1,2]
is
\{p,\neg q,\neg r,[D]\neg q,\psi_{1},\neg\psi_{2},\langle B\rangle\psi_{1},[D]\neg\psi_{2},\neg\varphi\}
, where
\psi_{1}=p\wedge\neg r
and
\psi_{2}=\neg q\wedge\langle D\rangle q
.

Since sets
\mathrm{Req}_{R}(F)
,
\mathrm{Obs}_{R}(F)
, and
\mathrm{Box}_{R}(F)
will be exploited to prove most of the results of the paper, we further illustrate their behavior by means of the example in Figure
6
.

{exa}

To start with, we observe that
\mathrm{Req}_{R}(F)
,
\mathrm{Obs}_{R}(F)
, and
\mathrm{Box}_{R}(F)
are univocally determined by their argument
F
. However, while
\mathrm{Obs}_{R}(F)\subseteq F
, this is not the case with
\mathrm{Req}_{R}(F)
and
\mathrm{Box}_{R}(F)
. With reference to the example in Figure
6
, it holds that
q\in\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[1,4]})
, but
q\notin F^{[1,4]}
.

Let us focus on
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)
, which extracts the arguments of the
\langle B\rangle
-formulas in
F
. In the considered example,
\psi_{1}(=p\wedge\neg r)\in\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,2]})
, as
\langle B\rangle\psi_{1}\in F^{[0,2]}
. Hence, there must be a proper prefix
[0,y]
of
[0,2]
such that
\psi_{1}\in F^{[0,y]}
(equivalently,
\psi_{1}\in\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[0,y]})
), and this is actually the case with
y=1
. In general, for each
\psi\in\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})
(resp.,
\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[x,y]})
), there must be a prefix
[x,y^{\prime}]
(resp., infix
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
) of
[x,y]
such that
\psi\in\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y^{\prime}]})
(resp.,
\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]})
). On the contrary, if
\langle R\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)
and
\psi\notin\mathrm{Req}_{R}(F)
, then, necessarily,
[R]\neg\psi\in F
and thus
\neg\psi\in\mathrm{Box}_{R}(F)
, i.e.,
\psi\not\in\mathrm{Box}_{R}(F)
.
Consider, for instance, the interval
[1,4]
in Figure
6
. We have that
\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[1,4]})=\{q\}
and, since
\{\psi:\langle D\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\}=\{q,\psi_{2}(=\neg q\wedge\langle D\rangle q)\}
, it holds that
\mathrm{Box}_{D}(F^{[1,4]})=\{\neg\psi_{2}\}
.

Finally, unlike the case of
\mathrm{Req}_{R}
, for every interval
[x,y]
, formula
\neg\psi\in\mathrm{Box}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})
(resp.,
\neg\psi\in\mathrm{Box}_{D}(F^{[x,y]})
),
and prefix
[x,y^{\prime}]
(resp., infix
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
) of
[x,y]
,
\psi\!\notin\!\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y^{\prime}]})
(resp.,
\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]})
).
As an example, since
\neg\psi_{2}\in\mathrm{Box}_{D}(F^{[1,4]})
, we can conclude that
\psi_{2}\notin\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[2,2]})\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[2,3]})\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[3,3]})
.

In order to further explain the relation between
\mathrm{Req}_{R}
and
\mathrm{Obs}_{R}
, let us consider the example in Figure
6
from another angle. Suppose that, for a fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
(in the example,
N=4
), one wants to find, for each
[x,y]
in
\mathbb{I}_{N}
, a “labelling”
F^{[x,y]}\in\mathrm{At}(\varphi)
such that

\begin{array}[]{lc}(*_{1})&\mathbf{M},[x,y]\models\psi\mbox{ if and only if }\psi\in F^{[x,y]},\end{array}

where
\mathbf{M}=(\mathbb{I}_{4},\mathcal{V})
and
\mathcal{V}([x,y])=F^{[x,y]}\cap\mathrm{Prop}
, and

\begin{array}[]{lc}(*_{2})&\varphi\in F^{[0,N]},\end{array}

Such a problem is the
bounded satisfiability problem
, which is simpler than the
finite satisfiability problem
we are addressing in this paper, where
N
is not given as a parameter.

It can be shown that the labelings that, for all
[x,y]\in\mathbb{I}_{N}
, satisfy the property:

\begin{array}[]{ll}(*_{3})&\begin{array}[]{l}\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})=\hskip-4.26773pt\bigcup\limits_{x\leq y^{\prime}<y}\hskip-4.26773pt\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y^{\prime}]})\mbox{ and }\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[x,y]})=\hskip 0.0pt\bigcup\limits_{x<x^{\prime}\leq y^{\prime}<y}\hskip 0.0pt\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]})\end{array}\end{array}

are
all and only
those labellings that satisfy property
(*_{1})
. Notice that these labellings may only differ on interval
[0,N]
.

This means that all the requests that we associate with an interval
[x,y]
by means of its labelling
F^{[x,y]}
must be satisfied (fulfilled). Consider, for instance, the prefix relation. It holds that
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})\subseteq\bigcup_{x\leq y^{\prime}<y}\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y^{\prime}]})
. Moreover, there cannot be
a formula
\psi
such that
\psi\in\bigcup_{x\leq y^{\prime}<y}\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y^{\prime}]})
and
\psi\notin\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})
, as, from the latter, it immediately follows
that
\neg\psi\in\mathrm{Box}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})
which implies
\psi\notin\bigcup_{x\leq y^{\prime}<y}\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y^{\prime}]})
(contradiction). This allows us to conclude that

\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})\supseteq\bigcup_{x\leq y^{\prime}<y}\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y^{\prime}]})
as well (consistency), and thus
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})=\bigcup_{x\leq y^{\prime}<y}\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y^{\prime}]})
. The same holds for modality
D
as well as for all the other modalities of
\mathsf{HS}_{hom}
. In fact, this is a fairly general property whose validity does not depend on the homogeneity assumption. Thus, we can conclude that
(*_{2})
and
(*_{3})
are
necessary and sufficient
conditions for
\mathbf{M}
to satisfy
\varphi
.

Let us now introduce two meaningful binary relations
\rightarrow_{B}
and
\rightarrow_{D}
over
\mathrm{At}(\varphi)
.

{defi}

For all
F,G\in\mathrm{At}(\varphi)
we let:

•

F\rightarrow_{B}G
iff
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)=\mathrm{Req}_{B}(G)\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(G)
;

•

F\rightarrow_{D}G
iff
\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F)\supseteq\mathrm{Req}_{D}(G)\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(G)
.

Relations
\rightarrow_{B}
and
\rightarrow_{D}
are often referred to as
view-to-type
dependencies since they constrain the labelling of an interval on the basis of the labellings of the intervals that it can access via certain (interval) relations. As already pointed out, for every
\psi\in\{\psi^{\prime}:\langle B\rangle\psi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\}
, either
\psi\in\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)
or
\neg\psi\in\mathrm{Box}_{B}(F)
(and vice versa). Given two atoms
F
and
G
, with
F\rightarrow_{B}G
, and a formula
\neg\psi\in\mathrm{Box}_{B}(F)
it immediately follows that
\psi\notin\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)
, and thus, from
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)=\mathrm{Req}_{B}(G)\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(G)
, it follows
\psi\notin\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(G)
.
Now, from
\neg\psi\in\mathrm{Box}_{B}(F)
, it follows that
\langle B\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)
, and from
\langle B\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)
and
\psi\notin\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(G)
, it follows that
\psi\notin G
. For the maximality of atoms, it follows that
\neg\psi\in G
. This allows us to conclude that for every pair of atoms
F
and
G
, with
F\rightarrow_{B}G
,
\mathrm{Box}_{B}(F)\subseteq G
.
The same argument can be applied to the relation
\rightarrow_{D}
, and thus for every pair of atoms
F
and
G
, with
F\rightarrow_{D}G
, it holds that
\mathrm{Box}_{D}(F)\subseteq G
. In addition, relation
\rightarrow_{D}
is transitive (by definition of atom, from
\mathrm{Req}_{R}(F)\supseteq\mathrm{Req}_{R}(G)
, it immediately follows that
\mathrm{Box}_{R}(F)\subseteq\mathrm{Box}_{R}(G)
), while
\rightarrow_{B}
is not. Finally, we call an atom which is
\rightarrow_{B}
-related to itself a
B
-reflexive atom (the notion of
D
-reflexive atom is defined in an analogous way). Atoms which are not
B
-reflexive are called
B
-irreflexive (
D
-irreflexive atoms are defined analogously). Reflexive atoms will play a crucial role in the proof of the results of the next sections.

An account of relations
\rightarrow_{B}
and
\rightarrow_{D}
is given by the examples in Figure
7
and Figure
8
, respectively. In Figure
7
(resp., Figure
8
), we denote
B
-reflexive (resp.,
D
-reflexive) atoms by self-loops.

\varphi=\langle B\rangle(
r\wedge\neg p\wedge\neg q
)\wedge\langle B\rangle(
\neg p\wedge\neg q\wedge\neg r
)\wedge\langle B\rangle\langle D\rangle(

Figure 7.
An account of the relation
\rightarrow_{B}
from both the interval point of view (left) and the spatial one (right).

{exa}

We exemplify the behavior of the relation
\rightarrow_{B}
by the example in Figure
7
. First of all, we observe that it constrains the relationship between the
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})
part of the labelling of an interval
[x,y]
and that of its maximal proper prefix
[x,y-1]
(if any). In any “consistent model”, for any strict-interval
[x,y]
, it indeed holds that
F^{[x,y]}\rightarrow_{B}F^{[x,y-1]}
(
\rightarrow_{B}
constrains the maximal prefix of an interval, not all its prefixes).
By “unravelling’ the definition of
\rightarrow_{B}
, we can show that the following three conditions are satisfied:

(1)

from
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y-1]})\subseteq\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})
, it follows that
\mathrm{Box}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})\subseteq\mathrm{Box}_{B}(F^{[x,y-1]})
, that is,
F^{[x,y-1]}
features at least the universal requests in
F^{[x,y]}
;

(2)

from
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y-1]})\subseteq\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})
, it follows that
F^{[x,y-1]}
possibly satisfies some of the requests in
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})
and it
does not violate any of the universal requests in
\mathrm{Box}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})
; otherwise, we would have
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y-1]})\setminus\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})\neq\emptyset
;

(3)

from
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})=\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y-1]})\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y-1]})
, we have that, for each
\psi\in\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})
, either
F^{[x,y-1]}
satisfies
\psi
(
\psi\in\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y-1]})
) or it delegates its satisfaction to its prefixes (
\psi\in\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y-1]})
). In the former case,
F^{[x,y-1]}
might potentially ask for
\psi
again (whenever
\psi\in\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y-1]})
as well). This is actually the behaviour that one may expect from the labelling of the maximal prefix
[x,y-1]
of an interval
[x,y]
: a request in
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]})
is either satisfied locally in
F^{[x,y-1]}
(possibly once and for all) or delegated to its prefixes.

In Figure
7
(both in the interval model to the left and in its graphical counterpart to the right), we show the labelling of the pairs of atoms/intervals that satisfy the relation
\rightarrow_{B}
. The example formula features three
\langle B\rangle
requests, namely,
\psi_{1}=r\wedge\neg p\wedge\neg q
,
\psi_{2}=\neg p\wedge\neg q\wedge\neg r
, and
\psi_{3}=\langle D\rangle\psi_{1}
. Let us focus our attention on the intervals starting at point
0
, namely,
[0,0],[0,1],[0,2],[0,3]
, and
[0,4]
. In Figure
7
(bottom), we report the atoms associated with these intervals, plus the one for the interval
[1,2]
, which satisfies the request
\langle D\rangle\psi_{1}
of intervals
[0,3]
and
[0,4]
.
In addition, at the very bottom of Figure
7
, we show when
F^{[0,x]}\rightarrow_{B}F^{[0,x^{\prime}]}
, with
0\leq x\leq x^{\prime}\leq 4
, is true and when it is not (a graphical account of the same pieces of information is given in the top right part of Figure
7
).

We now analyse the behavior of
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,x]})
and
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[0,x]})
for
x=0
up to
x=4
.

We first observe that both
F^{[0,0]}
and
F^{[0,1]}
satisfy neither
\psi_{1}
nor
\psi_{2}
(it suffices to observe that
p
holds on both of them). Moreover, both
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,0]})=\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,1]})=\emptyset
and
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[0,0]})=\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[0,1]})=\emptyset
, and
thus both
F^{[0,1]}\rightarrow_{B}F^{[0,0]}
and
F^{[0,0]}\rightarrow_{B}F^{[0,1]}
trivially hold, that is, the two labellings can be swapped without any consequence on the consistency of
B
requests. For the very same reason, it holds that
F^{[0,1]}\rightarrow_{B}F^{[0,1]}
as well as
F^{[0,0]}\rightarrow_{B}F^{[0,0]}
.

Atoms
F^{[0,0]}
and
F^{[0,1]}
are trivially
B
-reflexive because both their
B
-requests and their
B
-observables are empty. However, there may exist
B
-reflexive atoms
F
whose sets of
B
-requests and
B
-observables are both non-empty. A necessary and sufficient condition for an atom
F
to be
B
-reflexive is indeed that
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F)\subseteq\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)
. In such a case, the
B
-requests that
F
possibly satisfies by means of its observables are immediately reproduced by the
B
-requests themselves. This is the case with atom
F^{[0,4]}
in Figure
7
where both
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[0,4]})
and
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,4]})
are non-empty and

\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[0,4]})\subseteq\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,4]})
.

Atom
F^{[0,2]}
is a different story: it features
\psi_{1}
and thus
\psi_{1}\in\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[0,2]})
. However,
\psi_{1}\notin\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,2]})
and thus
F^{[0,2]}
is not
B
-reflexive. Clearly,
F^{[0,2]}\rightarrow_{B}F^{[0,1]}
, since both of them have no
\langle B\rangle
-requests.

Atom
F^{[0,3]}
is the first atom with at least one
\langle B\rangle
request, namely,
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,3]})=\{\psi_{1}\}
. It holds that
F^{[0,3]}\rightarrow_{B}F^{[0,2]}
, since
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,3]})=\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,2]})\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[0,2]})
(
\{\psi_{1}\}=\emptyset\cup\{\psi_{1}\}
). On the contrary,
F^{[0,3]}\rightarrow_{B}F^{[0,1]}
does not hold, since
F^{[0,1]}
neither features
\langle B\rangle\psi_{1}
nor satisfies
\psi_{1}
(
\{\psi_{1}\}\neq\emptyset\cup\emptyset
).
As for the observables, compared to
F^{[0,2]}
,
F^{[0,3]}
“loses”
\psi_{1}
, which is transferred to its
\langle B\rangle\psi_{1}
request, but both

\psi_{2}
and
\langle D\rangle\psi_{1}
are in
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[0,3]})
, and they may satisfy
\langle B\rangle
-requests coming from atoms labelling intervals that feature
[0,3]
as a proper prefix.

As for
F^{[0,4]}
,
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[0,4]})=\emptyset
and
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,4]})=\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\langle D\rangle\psi_{1}\}
. It can be easily checked that

F^{[0,4]}\rightarrow_{B}F^{[0,3]}
, i.e.,
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,4]})=\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,3]})\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[0,3]})
, as
\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\langle D\rangle\psi_{1}\}=\{\psi_{1}\}\cup\{\psi_{2},\langle D\rangle\psi_{1}\}
. By
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[0,3]})
,
\psi_{1}
is transferred to the proper prefixes of
[0,3]
, while both
\psi_{2}
and
\langle D\rangle\psi_{1}
are in
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[0,3]})
and thus are locally satisfied.

We conclude by noticing that there may be atoms
F
and
G
such that
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)=\mathrm{Req}_{B}(G)\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(G)
(i.e.,
F\rightarrow_{B}G
),
and
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(G)\cap\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(G)\neq\emptyset
, that is, a
\langle B\rangle
-request may be at the same time locally satisfied by
G
and featured as request for its proper prefixes.

The next important proposition determines, for any atom
F^{[x,y]}
, the number of atoms
F^{[x,y+k]}
, with
k\geq 1
and
(\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y+k]}),\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y+k]}))\neq(\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F^{[x,y]}),\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F^{[x,y]}))
, that
may have
F^{[x,y]}
as (the labeling of) a prefix. Its proof is given in Appendix
B
.

Proposition 1
.

Let
\varphi
be a
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formula. For any atom
F\in\mathrm{At}(\varphi)
and any sequence of atoms
F_{h}\rightarrow_{B}\ldots\rightarrow_{B}F_{1}\rightarrow_{B}F_{0}=F
, where, for each
0\leq i\neq j\leq h
,
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F_{i})\neq\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F_{j})
or
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F_{i})\setminus\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F_{i})\neq\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F_{j})\setminus\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F_{j})
, it holds that
h\leq 2|\{\psi:\langle B\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\}|-(2|\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)|+|\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F)\setminus\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)|)
.

Intuitively, Proposition
1
provides a linear bound on the number of distinct atoms
that may appear in a
\rightarrow_{B}
chain of atoms.

\varphi=\langle D\rangle(
p\wedge q
)\wedge\langle D\rangle(
\neg p\wedge q
)\wedge\langle D\rangle(
p\wedge\neg q
)
\psi_{1}
\psi_{2}
