.

Let us consider now relation
\rightarrow_{D}
. We first observe that, according to Definition
4
, given two atoms
F
and
G
, the condition imposed by
F\rightarrow_{D}G
is weaker than the one imposed by
\rightarrow_{B}
, that is, containement (superset) instead of full equality of the two sets. The reason is that by
F\rightarrow_{D}G
we state that
G
may label any sub-interval
[x,y]
of an interval
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
(
x^{\prime}<x\leq y<y^{\prime}
) and not just its maximal proper sub-interval
[x^{\prime}+1,y^{\prime}-1]
, while
G
in
F\rightarrow_{B}G
only refers to the maximal proper prefix
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}-1]
of
[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]
. We exemplify the behaviour of
\rightarrow_{D}
by means of the following example.

{exa}

In Figure
8
, we illustrate the labelling of intervals which are required to satisfy relation
\rightarrow_{D}
. We refer both to the interval model and to its graphical counterpart. Let us consider the following three
\langle D\rangle
-requests:
\psi_{1}=p\wedge q
,
\psi_{2}=\neg p\wedge q
, and
\psi_{3}=p\wedge\neg q
, and all the proper sub-intervals of the largest interval in the model, namely, sub-intervals
[1,1],[1,2],[1,3],[2,2],[2,3]
, and
[3,3]
of interval
[0,4]
. In Figure
8
(bottom), we report the cases where
F^{[x,y]}\rightarrow_{D}F^{[x^{\prime},y^{\prime}]}
, for
0<x^{\prime}\leq y^{\prime}<4
and
[x,y]=[0,4]
, is true and those where it is not. A graphical account of these relations is given in Figure
8
(top right).

Let us now describe the behavior of
\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[x,y]})
and
\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[x,y]})
moving from interval
[x,y]
to its maximal sub-interval
[x+1,y-1]
starting from the largest interval
[0,4]
. First, we observe that, since
\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[0,4]})=\emptyset
, it trivially holds that
\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[0,4]})\supseteq\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[0,4]})\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[0,4]})
, and thus
F^{[0,4]}\rightarrow_{D}F^{[0,4]}
, that is,
F^{[0,4]}
is
D
-reflexive. Hence, its labeling
F^{[0,4]}
may possibly be associated with a proper sub-interval of any interval labelled by
F^{[0,4]}
. The same holds with the
D
-reflexive atom
F^{[1,3]}
.
On the contrary, the relation
F^{[0,4]}\rightarrow_{D}F^{[1,3]}
cannot be turned into the relation
F^{[1,3]}\rightarrow_{D}F^{[0,4]}
, as
\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[1,3]})(=\{\psi_{1}\})\not\supseteq\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[0,4]})\cup\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[0,4]})(=\emptyset\cup\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\psi_{3}\})
.

It is worth pointing out that the following stronger consistency property, involving equality in place of containment, actually holds for
\langle D\rangle
-requests: for all
[x,y]
, it holds that

\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[x,y]})=\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[x+1,y]})\cup\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[x,y-1]})\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[x+1,y]})\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[x,y-1]})\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[x+1,y-1]})
.
It states that the
\langle D\rangle
-requests that hold over an interval
[x,y]
must be completely “covered” by those holding over its maximal proper prefix
[x+1,y]
, its maximal proper suffix
[x,y-1]
, and the union of all the observables of its maximal proper prefix, its maximal proper suffix, and its maximal proper sub-interval
[x+1,y-1]
.
As an example, in Figure
8
, we have that
\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[0,4]})=\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[1,4]})\cup\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F^{[0,3]})\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[1,4]})\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[0,3]})\cup\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(F^{[1,3]})=\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\}\cup\{\psi_{1},\psi_{3}\}\cup\emptyset\cup\emptyset\cup\emptyset=\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2},\psi_{3}\}
. Finally, notice that since both the maximal prefix
[x,y-1]
and the maximal suffix
[x+1,y]
) of an interval
[x,y]
are not proper sub-intervals of it, it may be the case that
F^{[x,y]}\rightarrow_{B}F^{[x,y-1]}
and/or
F^{[x,y]}\rightarrow_{B}F^{[x+1,y]}

do not hold
in a consistent model. For instance, in Figure
8
, neither
F^{[1,3]}\rightarrow_{D}F^{[1,2]}
nor
F^{[1,3]}\rightarrow_{D}F^{[2,3]}
holds.

The next proposition shows that to check the equality of atoms it suffices to check
the equality of their propositional components and of their respective sets of
\langle B\rangle
- and
\langle D\rangle
-requests.

Proposition 2
.

Let
F,G\in\mathrm{At}(\varphi)
. It holds that
F=G
if and only if
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)=\mathrm{Req}_{B}(G)
,
\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F)=\mathrm{Req}_{D}(G)
, and
F\cap\mathrm{Prop}=G\cap\mathrm{Prop}
.

The statement of Proposition
2
immediately follows from the fact that, for each atom
F
and each
\psi\in F
, either
\psi\in\mathrm{Prop}\cup\{\langle B\rangle\psi^{\prime}:\psi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)\}\cup\{\langle D\rangle\psi^{\prime}:\psi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F)\}
or
\psi
is a Boolean combination of
\mathrm{Prop}\cup\{\langle B\rangle\psi^{\prime}:\psi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)\}\cup\{\langle D\rangle\psi^{\prime}:\psi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F)\}
.

Given a formula
\varphi
, a
\varphi
-compass structure

(compass structure, when
\varphi
is clear from the context)
is a pair
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
, where
N\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{G}_{N}=\{(x,y):0\leq x\leq y\leq N\}
, and
\mathcal{L}:\mathbb{G}_{N}\rightarrow\mathrm{At}(\varphi)
is a
labelling function that satisfies the following conditions:

•

(
initial formula
)
\varphi\in\mathcal{L}(0,N)
;

•

(
B
-
consistency
) for all
0\leq x\leq y<N
,
\mathcal{L}(x,y+1)\rightarrow_{B}\mathcal{L}(x,y)
,
and for all
0\leq x\leq N
,
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(\mathcal{L}(x,x))=\emptyset
;

•

(
D
-
consistency
) for all
0\leq x<x^{\prime}\leq y^{\prime}<y\leq N
,
\mathcal{L}(x,y)\rightarrow_{D}\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
;

•

(
D
-
fulfilment
) for all
0\leq x\leq y\leq N
and all
\psi\in\mathrm{Req}_{D}(\mathcal{L}(x,y))
,
there exist
x<x^{\prime}\leq y^{\prime}<y
such that
\psi\in\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.

An example of compass structure is given in Figure
6
(top right), where the labelling with atoms of the second octant of a finite cartesian plane
is graphically depicted. Notice that the definition of
\rightarrow_{B}
and
B
-consistency guarantee that all
B
-requests are fulfilled.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure and let
\mathcal{P}:\mathbb{G}_{N}\rightarrow 2^{\mathrm{Prop}}
such that
\mathcal{P}(x,y)=\{p\in\mathrm{Prop}:p\in\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime})\mbox{ for all $x\leq x^{\prime}\leq y$}\}
. We say that
\mathcal{G}
is
homogeneous
if for all
(x,y)\in\mathbb{G}_{N}
,
\mathcal{L}(x,y)\cap\mathrm{Prop}=\mathcal{P}(x,y)
. The proof of the next theorem is straightforward and thus omitted.

Theorem 3
.

A
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formula
\varphi
is satisfiable
if and only if there is a homogeneous
\varphi
-compass structure.

Hereafter, we will often write compass structure for homogeneous

\varphi
-compass structure.

In the next sections, we will prove a small model theorem about compass structures for an input
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formula
\varphi
. In particular, we will prove that a model can be built by contracting a larger one in such a way that the resulting model is still a compass structure for
\varphi
. To this end, we need to preliminarily state some spatial properties of compass structures where the distinction between
B
-reflexive (resp.,
D
-reflexive) and
B
-irreflexive (resp.,
D
-irreflexive) atoms plays a major role. Intuitively, if a point is labelled with an atom which is both
B
-reflexive and
D
-reflexive, its only purpose is to “fill the gaps” in the model, as each
B
/
D
-request that it possibly solves for other points are transferred to its prefixes/sub-intervals. On the other hand, a
B
-irreflexive,
D
-irreflexive, or both
B
-irreflexive and
D
-irreflexive point must be dealt with carefully since its observables includes at least one
B
- or
D
-request that is solved once and for all, and it is not transferred to its prefixes/sub-intervals.

5.
Spatial properties of compass structures for

\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formulas

In this section, we state a series of spatial properties of compass structures that turn out to be quite useful to prove the results of Sections
6
and
7
.
Each property is proved by making use of the
previous one as follows.

In Section
5.1
, we show that for any compass structure and any of its
X
-axis coordinates
x
, the sequence
\mathcal{L}(x,0)\ldots\mathcal{L}(x,N)
is monotonic, i.e., for any triplet
0\leq y_{1}<y_{2}<y_{3}\leq N
, it cannot be the case that
\mathcal{L}(x,y_{1})=\mathcal{L}(x,y_{3})
and
\mathcal{L}(x,y_{2})\neq\mathcal{L}(x,y_{1})
. Such a property allows us to represent relevant information associated with any column
x
in space (polynomially) bounded in
|\varphi|
.
Next, in Section
5.2
, we define an equivalence relation over columns such that two columns are equivalent if they feature the same set of atoms. It is easy to check that such an equivalence relation is of finite index and its index is exponentially bounded in
|\varphi|
. By exploiting the representation of Section
5.1
, we first define a partial order over equivalent columns, and then we prove that, in a compass structure, such a relation totally orders them.
Finally, in Section
5.3
, by exploiting the total order of the elements of each equivalence class, we show a crucial property of the rows of a compass structure, which is the cornerstone of the proof. First, we associate with each point
(x,y)
on row
y
, with
0\leq x\leq y
, a tuple consisting of (i)
\mathcal{L}(x,y)
, (ii) the equivalence class
\sim_{x}
of column
x
, and (iii) the set of pairs
(\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},y),\sim_{x^{\prime}})
, for all

x<x^{\prime}\leq y
, and then we prove that, for every pair of points
(x,y),(x^{\prime},y)
that feature the same tuple,
\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
for all
y^{\prime}>y
, that is, columns
x
and
x^{\prime}
behave the same way (i.e., exhibit the same labelling) from
y
to the upper end.

5.1.
A finite characterisation of columns and of their relationships

In this section, we first show that, in every compass structure,
the atoms that appear in a column
x
must respect a certain order,
that is, they cannot be interleaved. Let
F,G,
and
H
be three
pairwise distinct atoms. In Figure
9
(a),
we give a graphical account of the property that we are going to prove, while, in Figure

9
(b), we show a violation of it (atom
H
appears before and after atom
G
moving upward along the column). To start with, we state a fundamental property of
B
-irreflexive atoms (the proof is given in Appendix
B
).

Lemma 4
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure.
For all
x\leq y<N
, if
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(\mathcal{L}(x,y))\subset\mathrm{Req}_{B}(\mathcal{L}(x,y+1))
, then
\mathcal{L}(x,y)
is
B
-irreflexive.

Observe that, from Lemma
4
, it follows that, given any sequence of points
(x,x)(x,x+1)\ldots(x,x+k)
in a compass structure, whenever we encounter a
B
-irreflexive atom, we have to drop at least one
B
-request, and thus the number of irreflexive atoms in the sequence is bounded by
|\{\langle B\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\}|
.

Pairing such an observation with the statement of Lemma
1
, it is possible to provide a bound on the number of distinct atoms that can be placed above a given atom
F
in a column, taking into account
B
-requests,
D
-requests, and negative literals in
F
.

Formally, we define a function
\Delta_{\uparrow}:\mathrm{At}(\varphi)\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
as follows:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\Delta_{\uparrow}(F)&=&(2|\{\langle B\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\}|-(2|\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)|+|\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F)\setminus\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)|))+\\
&&(|\{\langle D\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\}|-|\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F)|)+\\
&&(|\{\neg p:p\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\cap\mathrm{Prop}\}|-|\{\neg p:p\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\cap\mathrm{Prop}\wedge\neg p\in F\}|)\end{array}

The proof of Proposition
1
(see Section
4
)
helps us to understand why the factor
2
comes into play in the case of
B
-requestes.
Informally, from (the proof of) Proposition
1
, it immediately follows that, in order to move down from an atom featuring
\langle B\rangle\psi
to an atom featuring
\neg\psi,[B]\neg\psi
, one must pass through an atom featuring
\psi,[B]\neg\psi
.
It can be easily checked that, for each
F\in\mathrm{At}(\varphi)
, it holds that
0\leq\Delta_{\uparrow}(F)\leq 4|\varphi|+1
.

To illustrate how
\Delta_{\uparrow}
works, we give a simple example.

{exa}

Let
\{\psi:\langle B\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\}=\{\psi_{1}\}
and let
F_{3}\rightarrow_{B}F_{2}\rightarrow_{B}F_{1}
,
with
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F_{3})=\{\psi_{1}\}
and
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F_{2})=\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F_{1})=\emptyset
(by definition of
\rightarrow_{B}
, it immediately follows that
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F_{2})=\{\psi_{1}\}
and
\mathrm{Obs}_{B}(F_{3})=\emptyset
). For simplicity, let us assume that
\{\psi:\langle D\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\}=\emptyset
, and thus
\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F_{3})=\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F_{2})=\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F_{1})=\emptyset
, and

(F_{3}\cap F_{2}\cap F_{1})\cap\mathrm{Prop}=\mathrm{Prop}=\{p\}
.
It holds that
\Delta_{\uparrow}(F_{1})=(2\cdot 1-(2\cdot 0+0))+(0-0)+(1-0)=3
,
\Delta_{\uparrow}(F_{2})=(2\cdot 1-(2\cdot 0+1))+(0-0)+(1-0)=2
,
and
\Delta_{\uparrow}(F_{3})=(2\cdot 1-(2\cdot 1+0))+(0-0)+(1-0)=1
.

We say that an atom
F
is
initial
if and only
if
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)=\emptyset
. A
B
-sequence
is a sequence of atoms

\mathrm{Sh}_{B}=F_{0}\ldots F_{n}
such that
F_{0}
is initial
and for all
0<i\leq n
we have
F_{i}\rightarrow_{B}F_{i-1}
,

\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F_{i})\supseteq\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F_{i-1})
, and

F_{i}\cap\mathrm{Prop}\subseteq F_{i-1}\cap\mathrm{Prop}
.
It is worth pointing out that atoms in a
B
-sequence
are monotonically non-increasing
in
\Delta_{\uparrow}
, that is,
\Delta_{\uparrow}(F_{0})\geq\ldots\geq\Delta_{\uparrow}(F_{n})
.

{defi}

Let
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}=F_{0}\ldots F_{n}
be a
B
-sequence. We say that
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}
is
flat
if and only if it can be written as a sequence
\overline{F}^{k_{0}}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}^{k_{m}}_{m}
, where
k_{i}>0
, for all
0\leq i\leq m
, and
\overline{F}_{i}\neq\overline{F}_{j}
, for all
0\leq i<j\leq m
.

For the sake of clarity, it is worth mentioning that here and in the following
\overline{F}
it is not used to denote the complement of
F
, but as a simple alias for atoms.

We say that a flat
B
-sequence

\overline{F}^{k_{0}}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}^{k_{m}}_{m}
is
decreasing

if and only if
\Delta_{\uparrow}(\overline{F}_{0})>\ldots>\Delta_{\uparrow}(\overline{F}_{m})
. Flat (decreasing)
B
-sequences are the key ingredient of the following important lemmas. They indeed provide a suitable abstraction of the labelling of a sequence of intervals
[x,y_{1}],\ldots,[x,y_{n}]
with the same left endpoint. In particular, as we will show, if we ignore the
k_{i}
exponents, the representation of a flat (decreasing)
B
-sequence is bounded by the size of the input formula
\varphi
.

The following definition is at the basis of the abstraction of the sequence of intervals/points
[x,y_{1}],\ldots,[x,y_{n}]

of a compass structure into a flat (decreasing)
B
-sequence.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure for
\varphi
and

0\leq x\leq N
. We define the
shading of
x
in
\mathcal{G}
,
written
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)
, as the sequence of atoms
\mathcal{L}(x,x)\ldots\mathcal{L}(x,N)
.

A shading
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)
is nothing else but the word of atoms obtained “by reading the atoms along the column”

(x,x)\ldots(x,N)
from bottom to top in a compass structure. As an example, in Figure
6
,

\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(1)=F^{[1,1]}F^{[1,2]}F^{[1,3]}F^{[1,4]}
. The next lemma easily follows from Definition

5.1
and Definition
5.1
. It allows us to abstract the shadings in a compass
structure into flat
B
-sequences. The easy proof is omitted.

\vdots

F

F

G

H

H

H

\vdots

F\cap Prop\supseteq G\cap Prop\supseteq H\cap Prop

\mathrm{Req}_{B}(F)\subseteq\mathrm{Req}_{B}(G)\subseteq\mathrm{Req}_{B}(H)

\mathrm{Req}_{D}(F)\subseteq\mathrm{Req}_{D}(G)\subseteq\mathrm{Req}_{D}(H)

F

\vdots

F

G

H

H

H

\vdots

(a)
(b)
\vdots

F

F

H

H

G

H

\vdots

Figure 9.

(a)
Monotonicity of atoms along a column in a compass structure,
together with a graphical account of the corresponding intervals and
of how proposition letters and
B
- and
D
-requests
must behave.
(b)
An example of a violation of monotonicity.

Lemma 5
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure
and
0\leq x\leq N
. It holds that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)

is a
B
-sequence.

The next lemma is the missing piece that allows us to restrict our attention to decreasing flat
B
-sequences when abstracting shadings in a compass-structure.

Lemma 6
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure for a formula
\varphi
. For all
x\leq y<N
, it holds that
\mathcal{L}(x,y)=\mathcal{L}(x,y+1)
if and only if
\mathcal{L}(x,y)
is
B
-reflexive,
\mathrm{Prop}\cap\mathcal{L}(x,y)=\mathrm{Prop}\cap\mathcal{L}(x,y+1)
, and
\mathrm{Req}_{D}(\mathcal{L}(x,y))=\mathrm{Req}_{D}(\mathcal{L}(x,y+1))
.

The proof of Lemma
6
is given in Appendix
B
. From Lemma
6
,
it follows that, given a shading
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)=\mathcal{L}(x,x)\ldots\mathcal{L}(x,N)
, to change the atom moving from
(x,y)
to
(x,y+1)

we need to “sacrifice” at least one element in
\mathrm{Prop}\cap\mathcal{L}(x,y)
or to “acquire” one element in

\mathrm{Req}_{D}(x,y)
or in
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(x,y)
.

The next corollary immediately follows from Lemma
1
and Lemma
6
. It allows us to give a bound on the distinct atoms that may appear in a shading. More precisely, it states that the shading of each column
x
in
\mathcal{G}
is a flat decreasing
B
-sequence, and it gives a polynomial bound on the number of distinct atoms occurring in it.

Corollary 7
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure for a formula
\varphi
. Then, for all
0\leq x\leq N
,
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)
is a flat decreasing
B
-sequence
\overline{F}^{k_{0}}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}^{k_{m}}_{m}
, with
0\leq m\leq 4|\varphi|+1
.

5.2.
A suitable equivalence relation over columns of a compass structure

By exploiting the above (finite) characterisation of columns,
we can define a natural equivalence relation of finite index over columns.

First, we observe that, thanks to Corollary
7
,
if multiple copies of the same atom are present in a column, their occurrences are consecutive, and thus can be represented as blocks.
Moreover, if two columns feature the same set of atoms, the (blocks of) atoms appear in the same order in the two columns because of the monotonicity of
\mathrm{Req}_{B}
,
\mathrm{Req}_{D}
, and
Prop
, the latter being forced by the homogeneity assumption
(see Figure
9
(a)).

We will consider two columns
x,x^{\prime}
equivalent if and only if they feature the same set of atoms. Moreover, we will define a partial order relation over the shadings of equivalent columns that will allow us to totally order them. More precisely, for any two equivalent columns
x
and
x^{\prime}
, we will say that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)<\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
if and only if for every row
y
, with
y\geq x^{\prime}
, the atom
\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},y)
is equal to the atom
\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime})
for some row
y^{\prime}
, with
x\leq y^{\prime}\leq y
.
Intuitively, this means that moving up along column
x^{\prime}
an atom cannot appear until it has appeared on column
x
. In Fig.
10
(a), we depict two equivalent columns that satisfy such a condition.
In general, when moving upward, atoms on column
x^{\prime}
are often “delayed” with respect to atoms in column
x
, the limit case being when atoms on the same row are equal.
In Fig.
10
(b), a violation of the condition
(boxed atoms) is shown.
In the following, we will prove that the latter situation never occurs in a compass structure.

Let us now define an equivalence relation
\sim
over flat decreasing
B
-sequences as follows.

{defi}

Two flat decreasing
B
-sequences
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}=\overline{F}^{k_{0}}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}^{k_{m}}_{m}
and
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{\prime}=\overline{G}^{h_{0}}_{0}\ldots\overline{G}^{h_{m^{\prime}}}_{m^{\prime}}
are
equivalent
, written
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}\sim\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{\prime}
, if and only if
m=m^{\prime}
and, for all
0\leq i\leq m
,
\overline{F}_{i}=\overline{G}_{i}
.

Definition
5.2
says that two flat decreasing
B
-sequences are equivalent if and only if they feature exactly the same sequence of atoms regardless of their exponents. We choose as the representative of an equivalence class a flat decreasing
B
-sequence where each exponent is equal to one, e.g., the
B
-sequence
\overline{F}^{k_{0}}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}^{k_{m}}_{m}
belongs to the equivalence class
[\overline{F}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}_{m}]_{\sim}
.
Given an equivalence class
[\overline{F}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}_{m}]_{\sim}
and
0\leq i\leq m
, we denote by
[\overline{F}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}_{m}]^{i}_{\sim}
the
i^{th}
atom in its sequence, that is,
[\overline{F}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}_{m}]^{i}_{\sim}=\overline{F}_{i}
for all
0\leq i\leq m
.
In addition, we define a function
next
that, given an equivalence class
[\overline{F}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}_{m}]_{\sim}
and one of its atom
\overline{F}_{i}
,
returns the successor of
\overline{F}_{i}
in the sequence
[\overline{F}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}_{m}]_{\sim}
(for
i=n
, it is undefined).
It can be easily checked that
\sim
is of finite index. From Corollary
7
, it follows that its index
is (roughly) bounded by
|\mathrm{At}(\varphi)|^{4|\varphi|+2}=2^{(|\varphi|+1)(4|\varphi|+2)}=2^{4|\varphi|^{2}+6|\varphi|+2}
.

F_{1}

x

F_{2}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{4}

F_{4}

\vdots

F_{1}

x^{\prime}

F_{2}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{4}

\vdots
(a)
(b)

F_{1}

x

F_{2}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{4}

\vdots

F_{1}

x^{\prime}

F_{2}

F_{3}

F_{4}

F_{4}

\vdots

Figure 10.
Two equivalent columns that respect the order
(a)
and two equivalent columns that violate it
(b)
.

Let
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}=\overline{F}^{k_{0}}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}^{k_{m}}_{m}
be a flat decreasing
B
-sequence. We define the length of
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}
, written
|\mathrm{Sh}_{B}|
, as
\sum_{0\leq i\leq m}k_{i}
. A partial order
<
over the elements of each equivalence class
[\mathrm{Sh}_{B}]_{\sim}
can be defined as follows.

{defi}

Let
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}=\overline{F}^{k_{0}}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}^{k_{m}}_{m}
and
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{\prime}=\overline{F}^{h_{0}}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}^{h_{m}}_{m}
be two equivalent flat decreasing
B
-sequences. We say that
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}
is dominated by
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{\prime}
, written
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}<\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{\prime}
, if and only if (i)
|\mathrm{Sh}_{B}|>|\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{\prime}|
and, (ii) for all
0\leq i\leq m
,
\Sigma_{0\leq j\leq i}k_{j}\leq(|\mathrm{Sh}_{B}|-|\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{\prime}|)+\Sigma_{0\leq j\leq i}h_{j}
.

{exa}

Let us consider the equivalent flat decreasing
B
-sequences in Figure
10
. From left to right, they are

\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}=F_{1}F_{2}F^{3}_{3}F^{2}_{4}
,
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{1}=F_{1}F_{2}F^{2}_{3}F_{4}
,
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{2}=F_{1}F_{2}F^{4}_{3}F_{4}
, and
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{3}=F_{1}F_{2}F_{3}F^{2}_{4}
(for the sake of clarity, the exponent
1
is omitted).
Let us consider first
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}
and
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{1}
. By condition
(i)
of Definition
5.2
, the only possible domination relation is
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}<\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{1}
. Let us now check if condition
(ii)
of Definition
5.2
is satisfied.
To this end, let us consider the following representation of flat shadings. In general, whenever
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}
is equivalent to
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{1}
, in order to prove that
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}<\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{1}
, it suffices to take the alignment of
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{1}
(the shorter sequence) as a
suffix
of
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}
(the longer sequence). Such an alignment is obtained by prefixing
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{1}
with a word of length
|\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}|-|\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{1}|
only featuring the blank symbol ‘_​_’. In the example, the required alignment of
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{1}
is
\hat{\mathrm{Sh}}_{B}^{1}=\_\!\_^{2}F_{1}F_{2}F^{2}_{3}F_{4}
. It holds that
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}<\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{1}
if and only if the position of the first occurrence of each atom
F_{i}
in
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}
is not strictly smaller than that of the first occurrence of
F_{i}
in
\hat{\mathrm{Sh}}_{B}^{1}
. In the considered case, we have that:

•

F_{1}
occurs for the first time at position
0
in
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}

and at position
2
in
\hat{\mathrm{Sh}}_{B}^{1}
;

•

F_{2}
occurs for the first time at position
1
in
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}

and at position
3
in
\hat{\mathrm{Sh}}_{B}^{1}
;

•

F_{3}
occurs for the first time at position
2
in
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}

and at position
4
in
\hat{\mathrm{Sh}}_{B}^{1}
;

•

F_{4}
occurs for the first time at position
5
in
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}

and at position
6
in
\hat{\mathrm{Sh}}_{B}^{1}
.

Hence, we can conclude that
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}<\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{1}
. On the contrary, we have that
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{2}
is equivalent to
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{3}
, but
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{2}\not<\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{3}
. Indeed, if we consider the alignment
\hat{\mathrm{Sh}}_{B}^{3}=\_\!\_^{2}F_{1}F_{2}F_{3}F^{2}_{4}
,it holds that atom
F_{4}
occurs for the first time at position
5
in
\hat{\mathrm{Sh}}_{B}^{3}
and at position
6
in
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{2}
. Lemma
8
below proves that such a scenario cannot occur in the case of compass structures.

Let
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}=\overline{F}^{k_{0}}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}^{k_{m}}_{m}
be a flat decreasing
B
-sequence and let
0\leq i\leq|\mathrm{Sh}_{B}|
.
We introduce a notation for atom retrieval by letting
\mathrm{Sh}_{B}[i]=\overline{F}_{j}
, where
j
is such that
\sum_{0\leq{j^{\prime}}<j}k_{j^{\prime}}<i\leq\sum_{0\leq{j^{\prime}}\leq j}k_{j^{\prime}}
.
The next lemma constrains the relationships between pairs of equivalent shadings (flat decreasing
B
-sequences) that appear in a compass structure.

Lemma 8
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure.
For every pair of columns
0\leq x<x^{\prime}\leq N
such that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
, it holds that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)<\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
.

Proof 5.1
.

Let
\Delta=x^{\prime}-x
,
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)=\overline{F}^{k_{0}}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}^{k_{m}}_{m}
, and
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})=\overline{F}^{h_{0}}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}^{h_{m}}_{m}
. By contradiction, let us assume that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\not{<}\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
. From
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
, it follows that both
B
-sequences feature the same atoms in the same order; they may only differ in their numerousness, i.e., in the exponents of some atoms. Since
|\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)|>|\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})|
(
x^{\prime}
is closer to
N
than
x
and thus it is a shorter column), from
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
and
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\not{<}\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
, it follows that there exists
0<i\leq N-x^{\prime}
such that one of the following conditions holds:

(1)

\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[\Delta+i]\cap\mathrm{Prop}\supset\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i]\cap\mathrm{Prop}
;

(2)

\mathrm{Req}_{D}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[\Delta+i])\subset\mathrm{Req}_{D}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i])
;

(3)

\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[\Delta+i]
is
B
-irreflexive;

(4)

\mathrm{Req}_{B}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[\Delta+i])\subset\mathrm{Req}_{B}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i])
.

The above cases stem from the fact that we are assuming, by contradiction, that
for a certain index
i
there exists an index
j
such that

\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i]=\overline{F}_{j}
and
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[\Delta+i]=\overline{F}_{j-1}

(and thus
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i]\neq\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[\Delta+i]
).
This is the case, for instance, with
x
and
x^{\prime}

in Figure
10
(b), where
\Delta=2
,

\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[3]=F_{4}
, and
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[5]=F_{3}
.

Let
i
is the minimum index which satisfies one of the above conditions. In the following, we will assume
that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i]=\overline{F}_{j}
and
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[\Delta+i]=\overline{F}_{j-1}
for some
0<j\leq m
.

Before proving that all the above cases lead to a contradiction, we would like to explain how
they have been identified. Since
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
, but
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\not{<}\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
, and
|\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)|<|\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})|
, the situation is analogous to the one depicted in Figure
10
(b). By construction,
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)
is the first starting the unraveling of the sequence of atoms
\overline{F}_{0}\ldots\overline{F}_{m}
(
F_{1}\ldots F_{4}
in Figure
10
(b)), later followed by
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
, that unravels the same sequence of atoms (since
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
). Then,
either (i) for every
i
there exists
k^{\prime}\leq k
such that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i]=\overline{F}_{k^{\prime}}
and
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[i+\Delta]=\overline{F}_{k}
, i.e.,
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
“waits” for
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)
before displaying any new atom in the sequence,
or (ii) there exist
i
and
k^{\prime}>k
such that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i]=\overline{F}_{k^{\prime}}
and
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[i+\Delta]=\overline{F}_{k}
.
As an example, this is the case with
i=3
,
k=3
, and
k^{\prime}=4
in Figure
10
(b).
Condition (i) suffices to conclude that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)<\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
, while
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\not{<}\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
when condition (ii) holds.
One can easily check that if
i
is the minimal index that satisfies condition (ii), then
k^{\prime}=k+1
. Hence, it holds that
F_{k^{\prime}}\rightarrow_{B}F_{k}
, but
F_{k^{\prime}}\neq F_{k}
.

Cases (1)-(4) above are all the possible ways in which we may have
F_{k^{\prime}}\rightarrow_{B}F_{k}
and
F_{k^{\prime}}\neq F_{k}
, with the additional constraint that
F_{k^{\prime}}=\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime}+i)
and

F_{k^{\prime}}=\mathcal{L}(x,x+\Delta+i)
.

Let
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[i+\Delta](=\overline{F}_{j-1})=\mathcal{L}(x,x+\Delta+i)
and
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i](=\overline{F}_{j})=\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime}+i)
.

As for case (1), since the considered compass structures satisfy the homogeneity assumption and
[x^{\prime},x^{\prime}+i]
finishes
[x,x+\Delta+i]
, it holds that
\mathrm{Prop}\cap\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime}+i)\supseteq\mathrm{Prop}\cap\mathcal{L}(x,x+\Delta+i)
.
On the other hand, since
k^{\prime}>k
, there is
i^{\prime}>i
such that
\mathrm{Prop}\cap\mathcal{L}(x,x+\Delta+i^{\prime})=\mathrm{Prop}\cap\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime}+i)
,
and, since
[x,x+\Delta+i]
begins
[x,x+\Delta+i^{\prime}]
,
\mathrm{Prop}\cap\mathcal{L}(x+\Delta+i)\supseteq\mathrm{Prop}\cap\mathcal{L}(x,x+\Delta+i^{\prime})=\mathrm{Prop}\cap\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime}+i)
. It follows that
\mathrm{Prop}\cap\mathcal{L}(x+\Delta+i)=\mathrm{Prop}\cap\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime}+i)
(contradiction).

As for case (2), since
[x^{\prime},x^{\prime}+i]
is a proper suffix of
[x,x+\Delta+i]
, all the proper sub-intervals of
[x^{\prime},x^{\prime}+i]
are proper sub-intervals of
[x,x+\Delta+i]
as well, and,
since
\mathcal{G}
is a homogeneous compass structure,
\mathrm{Req}_{D}(\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},x^{\prime}+i))\subseteq\mathrm{Req}_{D}(\mathcal{L}(x,x+\Delta+i))
(contradiction).

As for case (3), let us assume that

\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[\Delta+i]=\mathcal{L}(x,x+\Delta+i)
is
B
-irreflexive.
From
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
and minimality of
i
, it follows that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[\Delta+i-1](=\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i-1])=\mathcal{L}(x,x+\Delta+i)=\overline{F}_{j-1}
.
From Lemma
6
(left-to-right direction), it immediately follows that
\mathcal{L}(x,x+\Delta+i)
is B-reflexive (contradiction).

Finally, let us assume
\mathrm{Req}_{B}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[\Delta+i])(=\overline{F}_{j-1})\subset\mathrm{Req}_{B}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i])(=\overline{F}_{j})
(case 4). We prove that such an assumption contradicts minimality of
i
. We distinguish two cases: either (i)
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i-1]=\overline{F}_{j-1}
or (ii)
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})[i-1]=\overline{F}_{j}
. In case (i), from Lemma
4
, it follows that
\overline{F}_{j-1}
is
B
-irreflexive. Hence,
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[\Delta+i-1]\neq\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[\Delta+i](=\overline{F}_{j-1})
, thus violating minimality of
i
.
In case (ii), it immediately follows that the minimum index must be less than or equal to
j-1
.

Even if it is susceptible to a quite straightforward spatial interpretation, the statement of
Lemma
8
is rather technical, and its role in the general picture may be a little obscure. As a matter of fact, it is an intermediate result that contributes a specific tile to the whole puzzle. The next example summarizes the properties of compass structures we proved so far, and gives a preview of the property we are going to demonstrate in the
next subsection.

{exa}

In Figure
11
, we represent the shading of
some columns belonging to the same equivalence class from a
certain row
y
up to the top row
N
of a compass structure.
More precisely, we consider 5 columns
x_{1},\ldots,x_{5}
,
with
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{1})\sim\ldots\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{5})
and

x_{1}<\ldots<x_{5}
, and a row
y\leq N
, with
x_{i}\leq y

for all
i\in\{1\ldots 5\}
. Then, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,5\}
,
we restrict our attention to the suffix of length
N-y
of
column
x_{i}
, that is,
Suf_{y}(x_{i})=\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{i})[y]\ldots\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{i})[N]
, for
i\in\{1,\ldots,5\}
.
In Figure
11
, we have

Suf_{y}(x_{1})=F_{3}^{4}F_{4}^{3}
,

Suf_{y}(x_{2})=F_{2}F_{3}^{4}F_{4}^{2}
,

Suf_{y}(x_{3})=Suf_{y}(x_{4})=F_{2}^{3}F_{3}^{2}F_{4}^{2}
, and

Suf_{y}(x_{5})=F_{2}^{5}F_{3}F_{4}
.

0

\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

y

\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

N

F_{3}

x_{1}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{4}

F_{4}

F_{4}

F_{2}

x_{2}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{4}

F_{4}

F_{2}

x_{3}

F_{2}

F_{2}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{4}

F_{4}

F_{2}

x_{4}

F_{2}

F_{2}

F_{3}

F_{3}

F_{4}

F_{4}

F_{2}

x_{5}

F_{2}

F_{2}

F_{2}

F_{3}

F_{4}

Figure 11.
A graphical account of the property stated
by Lemma
8
for suffixes of equal length belonging to columns in the same equivalence class.

First, we observe that a natural weakening of relation
<
of Definition
5.2
, where condition
(i)
is dropped,
can be exploited to compare suffixes of equal length. The resulting relation is no more asymmetric, and to distinguish it from the original one, we replace the symbol
<
by
\leq
. From Lemma
8
, it immediately follows that, in a compass structure,
Suf_{y}(x_{i})\leq Suf_{y}(x_{j})
whenever
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{i})\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{j})
and
x_{i}<x_{j}
.

In Figure
11
, the lowest occurrences of atoms
F_{3}
and
F_{4}
on each suffix have been connected by a broken line. We observe that, by moving from
x_{1}
to
x_{5}
, the two broken lines do not intersect, that is, the broken line for
F_{3}
is always below the broken line for
F_{4}
, and both are non-decreasing. This does not happen by chance, as we will prove in the next section (see Lemma
9
).
Moreover, Lemma
8
guarantees that identical suffixes are contiguous in the equivalence class, and can thus be grouped together as one unique block.
This is the case with
Suf_{y}(x_{3})
and
Suf_{y}(x_{4})
in Figure
11
.
These features of column suffixes will be exploited to obtain a finite characterization of rows bounded by some function of
|\varphi|
.

In general, the number of distinct suffixes depends on the distance
N-y
. Such a dependency may be problematic for a finite characterization of rows, as
N
can be arbitrary large. Think of a scenario where
y
is far away from
N
and all suffixes belong to the same equivalence class and differ from each other, that is,
Suf_{y}(0)<\ldots<Suf_{y}(y)
. Luckily, in the next section, we will prove that for every row
y
and every set of points
x_{1}<\ldots<x_{n}\leq y
, whose columns belong to the same equivalence class, the length of the maximum strictly ascending chain
Suf_{y}(x_{j_{1}})<\ldots<Suf_{y}(x_{j_{m}})
, with
\{j_{1},\ldots j_{m}\}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
(that is, the maximum number of distinct suffixes), is bounded by some function of
|\varphi|
.

5.3.
A spatial property of rows in homogeneous compass structures

We conclude the section by stating a covering property of points in a row of a compass structure that allows us provide a characterization of rows bounded in the size of the formula
\varphi
under consideration. A graphical account of such a property is given in Figure
12
.

To begin with, we introduce the fundamental notions of intersection and fingerprint.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure and let
0\leq x\leq y
. The intersection of row
y
and column
x
is the pair
([\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)]_{\sim},\mathcal{L}(x,y))
consisting of the equivalence class of
x
and the labelling of
(x,y)
.

The intersection
(x,y)
is basically the equivalence class of
x
, that is,
[\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)]_{\sim}
, where we have pinpointed the atom in
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)
that appears at height
y
. As an example, in the example of Figure
6
, the intersection of
(1,3)
is the
pair
([F^{[1,1]}F^{[1,2]}F^{[1,3]}F^{[1,4]}]_{\sim},F^{[1,3]})
.

Let us now define the notion of fingerprint for a given a point
(x,y)
, which basically pairs the intersection of row
y
and column
x
with the set of intersections of row
y
and column
x^{\prime}
, for all
x<x^{\prime}\leq y
, that is, the intersections of row
y
with the columns
occurring to the “right” of column
x
. Formally, let
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y)
be the set
\{([\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})]_{\sim},\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},y)):x^{\prime}>x\}
. As an example, in Figure
6
, we have that
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(1,3)=\{([F^{[2,2]}F^{2,3}F^{[2,4]}]_{\sim},F^{[2,3]}),([F^{[3,3]}F^{[3,4]}]_{\sim},F^{[3,3]})\}
. As a general rule,
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y)
collects the equivalence classes of
\sim
which are witnessed to the right of
x
on row
y
plus a “pointer” to the “current atom”, that is, the atom they are exposing on
y
.
If
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
is homogeneous (as it is in our setting), for all
0\leq x\leq y\leq N
, the number of possible sets
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y)
is bounded by
2^{2^{4|\varphi|^{2}+6|\varphi|+2}\cdot 2^{|\varphi|+1}}=2^{2^{4|\varphi|^{2}+7|\varphi|+3}}
, that
is, it is doubly exponential in the size of
|\varphi|
. Such a bound is obtained as follows. The number of possible equivalence classes for columns, that is, the number of possible flat decreasing
B
-sequences, is
2^{4|\varphi|^{2}+6|\varphi|+2}
and the number of possible atoms is
2^{|\varphi|+1}
. Their product gives a bound on the number of pairs
([\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})]_{\sim},\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},y))
. Then, the number of all possible sets on such pairs is bounded by
2^{2^{4|\varphi|^{2}+6|\varphi|+2}\cdot 2^{|\varphi|+1}}
.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure and let
0\leq x\leq y
. The fingerprint of
(x,y)
, denoted by
fp(x,y)
, is the triplet
consisting of the intersection of row
y
and column
x
plus the set
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y)
, that is,
fp(x,y)=([\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)]_{\sim},\mathcal{L}(x,y),\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y))
.

As an example, the fingerprint of row
3
and column
1
in Figure
6
is:

fp(1,3)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}[F^{[1,1]}F^{[1,2]}F^{[1,3]}F^{[1,4]}]_{\sim},F^{[1,3]},\\
\{([F^{[2,2]}F^{[2,3]}F^{[2,4]}]_{\sim},F^{[2,3]}),([F^{[3,3]}F^{[3,4]}]_{\sim},\allowbreak F^{[3,3]})\}\end{array}\right).

The next lemma constrains the way in which two columns
x,x^{\prime}
, with
x<x^{\prime}
and
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})
,
evolve from a given row
y
on when
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y)=\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x^{\prime},y)
.

\cdots

y

y^{\prime}

\cdots

N

x

\cdots

x_{0}

\cdots

x_{n}

\cdots
\sim
\sim

\ldots

\sim

F

\ldots

F

\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}(x_{0},y^{\prime})

F

\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{n},y)

\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{i},y)

\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{0},y)

\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y)

\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y)=\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{0},y)=\ldots=\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{i},y)=\ldots=\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{n},y)

Figure 12.
A graphical account of the behaviour of covered points:
x
is covered by
x_{0}<\ldots<x_{n}
on row
y
, and thus the labelling of points on column
x
above
(x,y)
is exactly the same as that of the corresponding points on column
x_{0}
above
(x_{0},y)
, that is,
\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}(x_{0},y^{\prime})
, for all
y\leq y^{\prime}\leq N
.

Lemma 9
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure and let

0\leq x<x^{\prime}\leq y\leq N
. If
fp(x,y)=fp(x^{\prime},y)

and
y^{\prime}
is the smallest point greater than
y
such that
\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime})\neq\mathcal{L}(x,y)
, if any, and
N
otherwise,
then, for all
y\leq y^{\prime\prime}\leq y^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})
.

From Lemma
9
, the next corollary easily follows.

Corollary 10
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure and let

0\leq x<x^{\prime}\leq y\leq N
.
If
fp(x,y)=fp(x^{\prime},y)
and
y^{\prime}
is the smallest point greater than
y
such that
\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime})\neq\mathcal{L}(x,y)
, if any, and
N
otherwise,
then, for every pair of points
\overline{x},\overline{x}^{\prime}
, with

x<\overline{x}<x^{\prime}<\overline{x}^{\prime}
, with
\mathcal{L}(\overline{x},y)=\mathcal{L}(\overline{x}^{\prime},y)
and

\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(\overline{x})\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(\overline{x}^{\prime})\not\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)
, it holds that
\mathcal{L}(\overline{x},y^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{L}(\overline{x}^{\prime},y^{\prime\prime})
, for all
y\leq y^{\prime\prime}\leq y^{\prime}
.

The above results lead us to the identification of those points
(x,y)
whose behaviour
perfectly reproduces that of a number of points
(x^{\prime},y)

on their right with
fp(x,y)=fp(x^{\prime},y)
.
These points
(x,y)
, like all points “above” them, are useless with respect to fulfilment in a compass structure. We call them
covered points
.

{defi}

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure and let
0\leq x\leq y\leq N
. We say that
(x,y)

is a
covered
point if and only if there exist
n+1=\Delta_{\uparrow}(\mathcal{L}(x,y))
distinct points
x_{0}<\ldots<x_{n}\leq y
, with
x<x_{0}
, such that for all
0\leq i\leq n
,
fp(x,y)=fp(x_{i},y)
.
In such a case, we say that
x
is covered by
x_{0}<\ldots<x_{n}
on
y
.

The following lemma holds.

Lemma 11
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure and let
0\leq x\leq y\leq N
be such that
x
is covered by
x_{0}<\ldots<x_{n}
on
y
. Then, for all
y\leq y^{\prime}\leq N
, it holds that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)[y^{\prime}]=\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{0})[y^{\prime}]
.

Proof 5.2
.

Let
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x,y)=F^{k_{0}}_{0}\ldots F^{k_{m}}_{m}
. The proof is by induction on
n=\Delta_{\uparrow}(\mathcal{L}(x,y))
.

If
n=0
, then
\mathcal{L}(x,y)=F_{m}
, and since
\mathcal{L}(x,y)=\mathcal{L}(x_{0},y)
,
F_{m}=\mathcal{L}(x_{0},y)
. Since we are on the last atom of the sequence
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x,y)
and
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x,y)\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{0},y)
, it holds that
\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}(x_{0},y^{\prime})
for all
y<y^{\prime}\leq N
.

If
n>0
, let
\mathcal{L}(x,y)=F_{i}
, with
0\leq i<m
(if
i=m
, we can follow the same reasoning path as of the inductive basis). By Lemma
9
, there exists a minimum point
y^{\prime}>y
such that
\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}(x_{0},y^{\prime})=\ldots=\mathcal{L}(x_{n},y^{\prime})=F_{i+1}
,
and thus, for all
y\leq y^{\prime\prime}\leq y^{\prime}
, it holds that
\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{L}(x_{0},y^{\prime\prime})
.
Moreover, by Corollary
10
, for every
\overline{x}>x_{n}
such that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(\overline{x})\not\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)
if
there exists
x<\overline{x}^{\prime}<x_{n}
such that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(\overline{x}^{\prime})\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(\overline{x})
and
\mathcal{L}(\overline{x},y)=\mathcal{L}(\overline{x}^{\prime},y)
, it holds that
\mathcal{L}(\overline{x},y^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}(\overline{x}^{\prime},y^{\prime})
.
Then, it follows that
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y^{\prime})=\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{i},y^{\prime})
for every
0\leq i<n
(every one but
x_{n}
). Since
\Delta_{\uparrow}(F_{i})<\Delta_{\uparrow}(F_{i+1})
,
and
x
is covered by
x_{0}<\ldots<x_{n-1}
on
y^{\prime}
, we can apply the inductive hypothesis and conclude that, for every
y^{\prime}\leq y^{\prime\prime}\leq N
,
\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime\prime})=\mathcal{L}(x_{0},y^{\prime\prime})
.

In Figure
13
, we give an intuitive account of the notion of covered point and of the statement of Lemma
11
.
First of all, we observe that, since
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y)=\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{0},y)=\ldots=\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{n},y)
and, for all
0\leq j,j^{\prime}\leq n
, it holds that
(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{j}),\mathcal{L}(x_{j},y))=(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{j^{\prime}}),\mathcal{L}(x_{j^{\prime}},y))
, there exists
x_{n}<\hat{x}\leq y
such that
\hat{x}
is the smallest point greater than
x_{n}
that satisfies the condition
(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{n}),\mathcal{L}(x_{n},y))=(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(\hat{x}),

\mathcal{L}(\hat{x},y))
.
Now, it can be the case that
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{n},y)\supset\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(\hat{x},y)
, and all points
\overline{x}^{\prime}>x_{n}
such that
(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(\overline{x}^{\prime}),\mathcal{L}(\overline{x}^{\prime},y))=(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(\overline{x}),\mathcal{L}(\overline{x},y))
, for some
x<\overline{x}<x_{n}
, are in between
x_{n}
and
\hat{x}
, that is,
x_{n}<\overline{x}^{\prime}<\hat{x}
. Then, it may happen that, for all
0\leq i\leq n
,
\mathcal{L}(x_{i},y^{\prime})=F_{i+1}
, as all points
(x_{i},y^{\prime})
satisfy some
D
-request
\psi
that only belongs to
\mathcal{L}(\overline{x}^{\prime},y^{\prime}-1)
.
In such a case, as shown in Figure
13
,
\mathcal{L}(\hat{x},y^{\prime})=F_{i}
, because for all points

(\hat{x}^{\prime},\hat{y}^{\prime})
, with
\hat{x}<\hat{x}^{\prime}\leq\hat{y}^{\prime}<y^{\prime}
,
\psi\notin\mathcal{L}(\hat{x}^{\prime},\hat{y}^{\prime})
.
Hence,
(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{n}),F_{i+1})\in\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{j},y^{\prime})
for all
0\leq j<n
, but
(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x_{n}),F_{i+1})\notin\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{n},y^{\prime})
. Then, by applying Corollary
10
, we have that
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{0},y^{\prime})=\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x_{n-1},y^{\prime})
. Since

\Delta_{\uparrow}(F_{i+1})<\Delta_{\uparrow}(F_{i})(=n)
, it holds that
\Delta_{\uparrow}(F_{i+1})\leq n-1

The same argument can then be applied to
x,x_{0},\ldots,x_{n-1}
on
y^{\prime}
, and so on.

6.
The satisfiability problem for

\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
belongs to
ExpSpace

In this section, by exploiting the properties proved in Section
5
, we show that the problem of checking whether a
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formula
\varphi
is satisfied by some homogeneous model can be decided in exponential space.
First, by means of a suitable small model theorem, we prove
that either
\varphi
is unsatisfiable or it is satisfied
by a model (a compass structure) of at most doubly-exponential size in
|\varphi|
; then, we show that this model of doubly-exponential size can be guessed in single exponential space.

Theorem 12
.

The problem of deciding whether or not a
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formula
\varphi
is satisfiable, over finite linear orders, belongs to
ExpSpace
.

The proof of Theorem
12
follows from
Corollary
13
, Lemma
14
, and Lemma
15
below.

First of all, thanks to the property proved in Section
5.3
, we know that, for every row
y
, there is a
bounded number of columns
C_{y}=\{x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
that may behave pairwise differently for the portion of the compass structure above
y
, where
n
only depends on
|\varphi|
. This means that each column
0\leq x\leq y
, with
x\notin C_{y}
, behaves exactly as some
x_{i}\in C_{y}

above
y
, that is, for all
y^{\prime}>y
,
\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}(x_{i},y^{\prime})
.
Moroever, by exploiting Lemma
11
, we can show that,
for each row
y
, the cardinality of the set of columns
x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{m}
which are not covered on
y
is at most exponential in
|\varphi|
.
Then, the bound on the length of the sequence of triplets for non-covered points that appear on
y
is exponential in
|\varphi|
.
The latter claim stems from the fact that the third component
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y)
of each triplet

fp(x,y)=([\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)]_{\sim},\mathcal{L}(x,y),\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y))
satisfies the condition
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y)\subseteq\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x^{\prime},y)

for all
x^{\prime}\leq x
.
Thanks to such a monotonic behavior of the third component of fingerprints on any row
y
, there are at most
an exponential number of distinct
\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y)
. Finally the number of identical non-covered points associated with the same triplet
([\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)]_{\sim},\mathcal{L}(x,y),\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y))
is (polynomially) bounded by
\Delta_{\uparrow}(\mathcal{L}(x,y))
.

The set of possible characterizations of rows in a compass structure may thus vary in the set of possible sequences of triplets, which are exponentially long in
|\varphi|
. Since the latter can be viewed as symbols of a finite alphabet, whose cardinality is exponential in
|\varphi|
, we can conclude that the number of distinct characterizations/sequences is bounded by a double exponential in
|\varphi|
.
It immediately follows that, in a compass structure whose size is more than doubly exponential in
|\varphi|
, there exist two rows
y,y^{\prime}
, with
y<y^{\prime}
, such that the sequences of the triplets for non-covered points that appear on
y
and
y^{\prime}
are exactly the same. This allows us to apply a “contraction” between
y
and
y^{\prime}
on the compass structure.

An example of how contraction works is given in Figure
14
.
First of all, notice that rows
7
and
11
feature the same sequences for triplets of non-covered points.
Notice that the two sequences are the same even though the second occurrence (from the right) of
(Sh^{\prime},F_{2})

precedes the second occurrence (from the right) of
(Sh,F_{3})
on row
7
and follows it on row 11.
Moreover, on any row, each covered point is connected by an edge to the non-covered point that “behaves” in the same way.
More precisely, we have that column
2
behaves as column
4
between
y=7
and
y^{\prime}=15
, columns
3,5
, and
7
behave as column
8
between
y=11
and
y^{\prime}=15
,
and column
4
behaves as column
6
between
y=11
and
y^{\prime}=15
.
The compass structure in Figure
14
.(a) can thus be shrinked into the compass structure in Figure
14
.(b), where
each column of non-covered points
x
on
y^{\prime}
is copied
above the corresponding non-covered point
x^{\prime}
on
y
. Moreover,
the column of a non-covered point
x
on
y^{\prime}
is copied
over all the points which are covered by the non-covered point
x^{\prime}
corresponding to
x
on
y
. This is the case with point
2
in Figure
14
.(b)
which takes the new column of its “covering” point
4
.
The resulting compass structure is
y^{\prime}-y
shorter than the original one, and we can repeatedly apply such a contraction until we achieve the desired bound.

The next corollary, which easily follows from Lemma
11
,
is crucial for the proof of Theorem
12
. It basically states that the property of “being covered” propagates upward.

An example of contraction, where compass structure (a) is contracted into compass structure (b).

Corollary 13
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure. Then, for every covered point
(x,y)
, it holds that, for all
y\leq y^{\prime}\leq N
, point
(x,y^{\prime})
is covered as well.

From Corollary
13
, it immediately follows that, for every covered point
(x,y)
and every
y\leq y^{\prime}\leq N
, there exists
x^{\prime}>x
such that
\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}(x,y^{\prime})
.
Hence, for all
\overline{x},\overline{y}
, with
\overline{x}<x\leq y^{\prime}<\overline{y}
,
and any
D
-request
\psi\in\mathrm{Req}_{D}(\mathcal{L}(\overline{x},\overline{y}))\cap\mathrm{Obs}_{D}(\mathcal{L}(x,y))
, we have that
\psi\in\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},y)
, with
x^{\prime}>x
. This allows us to conclude that if
(x,y)
is covered, then all points
(x,y^{\prime})
, with
y^{\prime}\geq y
,
are “useless” from the point of view of
D
-requests.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure and
0\leq y\leq N
. We define the set of
witnesses
of
y
as the set

\mathrm{Wit}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)=\{x:(x,y)\mbox{ is not covered}\}
.
Corollary
13
guarantees that, for any row
y
, the shading
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)
and the labelling
\mathcal{L}(x,y)
of witnesses
x\in\mathrm{Wit}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)
are sufficient, bounded, and unambiguous pieces of information that one needs to maintain about
y
.

Given a compass structure
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
and
0\leq y\leq N
, we define the
row blueprint
of
y
in
\mathcal{G}
,
written
\mathrm{Row}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)
, as the sequence
\mathrm{Row}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)=([\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{0}]_{\sim},F_{0})\ldots([\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{m}]_{\sim},F_{m})
such that
m+1=|\mathrm{Wit}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)|
and there exists a bijection
b:\mathrm{Wit}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)\rightarrow\{0,\ldots,m\}
such that, for every
x\in\mathrm{Wit}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)
, it holds that
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\in[\mathrm{Sh}_{B}^{b(x)}]_{\sim}
and
F_{b(x)}=\mathcal{L}(x,y)
, and for every
x,x^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Wit}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)
,
b(x)<b(x^{\prime})\leftrightarrow x<x^{\prime}
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure. The next lemma proves that if
\mathcal{G}
features two distinct rows
y<y^{\prime}
which share the same blueprint, then there exists a compass structure
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N^{\prime}},\mathcal{L}^{\prime})
with
N^{\prime}<N
.

Lemma 14
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
be a compass structure. If there exist two points
y,y^{\prime}
, with
0\leq y<y^{\prime}\leq N
, such that

\mathrm{Row}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)=\mathrm{Row}_{\mathcal{G}}(y^{\prime})
, then there exists a compass structure
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathbb{G}_{N^{\prime}},\mathcal{L}^{\prime})
with
N^{\prime}=N-(y^{\prime}-y)
.

Proof 6.1
.

From
\mathrm{Row}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)=\mathrm{Row}_{\mathcal{G}}(y^{\prime})
, by composing bijections,it follows that
there exists a bijection
\overline{b}:\mathrm{Wit}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)\rightarrow\mathrm{Wit}_{\mathcal{G}}(y^{\prime})

such that, for every
x\in\mathrm{Wit}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)
,
\mathcal{L}(x,y)=\mathcal{L}(\overline{b}(x),y^{\prime})
,
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x)\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(\overline{b}(x))
, and

\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x,y)=\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(\overline{b}(x),y^{\prime})
. Moreover, for every
x,x^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Wit}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)
,

x\leq x^{\prime}\leftrightarrow\overline{b}(x)\leq\overline{b}(x^{\prime})
.
For every point
0\leq x\leq y
, we define the function

\mathrm{Closest}_{\mathrm{wit}}:\{0,\ldots,y\}\rightarrow\{0,\ldots,y\}

as follows:

\mathrm{Closest}_{\mathrm{wit}}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}x&\mbox{if $x\in\mathrm{Wit}_{\mathcal{G}}(y)$}\\
min\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}x^{\prime}:x^{\prime}>x,x^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Wit}_{\mathcal{G}}(y),\mathcal{L}(x^{\prime},y)=\mathcal{L}(x,y),\\
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x^{\prime})\sim\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathcal{G}}(x),\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x^{\prime})=\mathbb{S}_{\rightarrow}(x)\end{array}\right\}&\mbox{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Let
\delta=y^{\prime}-y
.
We define
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
as follows:

(1)

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\overline{x},\overline{y})=\mathcal{L}(\overline{x},\overline{y})
,
for all
0\leq\overline{x}\leq\overline{y}\leq y
;

(2)

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\overline{x},\overline{y})=\mathcal{L}(\overline{x}+\delta,\overline{y}+\delta)
,
for all
y<\overline{x}\leq\overline{y}\leq N
;

(3)

\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(\overline{x},\overline{y})=\mathcal{L}(\mathrm{Closest}_{\mathrm{wit}}(\overline{x}),\overline{y}+\delta)
,
for all points
(x,y)
with

0\leq\overline{x}\leq y
and
y<\overline{y}\leq N
.
To complete the proof it suffices to show that the resulting structure

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}=(\mathbb{G}_{N^{\prime}},\mathcal{L}^{\prime})
is a homogeneous compass structure. This part is
omitted, since it is pretty simple, but extremely long (it can be proved by exploiting
Corollary
13
and the definition of witnesses for a row
y
).

To conclude the proof of Theorem
12
, we only need to show that if a
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formula is satisfiable, then it is satisfied by a doubly exponential compass structure, whose existence can be checked in exponential space. The following lemma provides both the
small model
theorem and the
ExpSpace
membership.

Lemma 15
.

Let
\varphi
be a
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
formula. It holds that
\varphi
is satisfiable if and only if there is a compass structure
\mathcal{G}=(\mathbb{G}_{N},\mathcal{L})
for it
such that
N\leq 2^{2(|\varphi|+1)(4|\varphi|^{2}+7|\varphi|+3)2^{8|\varphi|^{2}+14|\varphi|+6}}
, whose existence can be checked in
ExpSpace
.

The proof of Lemma
15
can be found in Appendix
B
. To compute the bound, it heavily relies on the small model property stated by Lemma
14
, while the argument of the proof of the
ExpSpace
membership is a suitable adaptation of the classical on-the-fly reachability check used, for instance, to prove that
\mathsf{LTL}
satisfiability belongs to
PSpace

[
CVWY92
]
.

7.
The satisfiability problem for

\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
is decidable in
ExpSpace

In this section, we focus on the logic
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
, that extends
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
with modality
\langle A\rangle
. We first specify its syntax and semantics; then, we go through the definitions and proofs of Sections
5.1
,
5.2
,
and
5.3
, and show what changes must be done to transfer them from
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
to
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
.

Formally, syntax and semantics of
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
are obtained from those of
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
by simply adding the syntactic rule and the semantic clause for modality
\langle A\rangle
, respectively.

\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
formulas are built up from a countable set
\mathrm{Prop}
of proposition letters according to the grammar:

\varphi::=p\ |\ \neg\psi\ |\ \psi\vee\psi\ |\ \langle B\rangle\psi\ |\ \langle D\rangle\psi\ |\ \langle A\rangle\psi.

The semantics of a
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
formula is specified by the semantic clauses for
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
, given in Section
3
, plus the following one:

•

\mathbf{M},[x,y]\models\langle A\rangle\psi
iff
there is
y^{\prime}
, with
y^{\prime}\geq y
, such that

\mathbf{M},[y,y^{\prime}]\models\psi
.

Hereafter, we will denote by
TF^{\varphi}_{A}
the set
\{\psi:\langle A\rangle\psi\in\mathrm{Cl}(\varphi)\}
.

Notice that modality
\langle A\rangle
allows one to refer to the formulas that hold at the right endpoint of an interval. As an example, to force formula
\psi
to hold at the right endpoint of the current interval
[x,y]
(point-interval
[y,y]
), it suffices to
state that
\langle A\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi)
holds at
[x,y]
(it is useful to remark that
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
already allowed us to constrain a formula
\psi
to hold at the left endpoint
[x,x]
of an interval
[x,y]
by means of the formula
\langle B\rangle(\pi\wedge\psi)
).

Such an ability to force formulas to hold at the left or the right endpoint of an interval makes a significant difference between
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
and
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
. It will play a crucial role in proving that the
ExpSpace
complexity bound for the
satisfiability problem of
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
is tight. In fact, it is possible to show that it is the only capability that we need
to add to
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
to prove
ExpSpace
hardness (to make it clear, we would need to suitably rewrite the encoding of Section
8
into a lengthier one).
2
2

2

In
[
BMPS21b
]
, the satisfiability problem for
\mathsf{BD}_{hom}
has been shown to be
PSpace-complete
.

In Appendix
C
, we give an example of a model of a
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
formula that make use of all the three operators of
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
. Moreover, in Appendix
D
, we show that
\textsf{BDA}_{hom}
may encode a very expressive fragment of generalized
*
-free regular expressions that features prefix, infix, and lookahead. In contrast to its non-elementarily-hard complexity in the case of full generalized
*
-free regular expressions
[
Sto74
]
, the emptiness problem turns out to be
ExpSpace-complete
for such a fragment.

Let us now prove decidability of
\mathsf{BDA}_{hom}
in
ExpSpace
. To begin with, we state a lemma that establishes a basic property of modality
\langle A\rangle
, which will be extensively used in the following definitions and proofs.

Lemma 16
.

For every interval structure
\mathbf{M}=(\mathbb{I}_{N},\mathcal{V})
,
every triplet of points
x\leq y\leq z
in

\{0,\ldots,N\}
, and every
\mathsf{HS}
formula
\psi

\mathbf{M},[x,z]\models\langle A\rangle\psi
if and only if

\mathbf{M},[y,z]\models\langle A\rangle\psi
.

A proof of Lemma
16
can be found in
[
BMS07
]
. Here we give a graphical account of it in Figure

, which shows that intervals (resp., points) sharing their right endpoint (resp., laying on the same row) must feature the same
\mathsf{A}
-requests.

A graphical account of the statement of Lemma
16
from both an interval point of view and a spatial one.

Before moving to formal definitions, let us dig deeper in the consequences of Lemma
16
, the constraints that can be imposed by means of modality
\langle A\rangle
in a compass structure, and the way in which we can deal with them by extending the labelling of a compass structure.

First, for every row
y
in a compass structure, Lemma
16
states that all the points
(0,y)\ldots(y,y)
share the very same set of
\mathsf{A}
-requests, which must be satisfied on the column starting in
y
, that is, by points
(y,y)\ldots(y,N)
.
Intuitively, if we consider the
90
degree angle with vertex in
(y,y)
we have a set of
\mathsf{A}
-requests associated with its
horizontal edge which must be satisfied on its vertical edge. Notice that modality
\langle A\rangle
alone is not able to constrain the
order according to which requests have to be satisfied on column
x
. To impose it, a suitable combination of modalities
\langle A\rangle
and
\langle B\rangle
is necessary.

Let us focus our attention on the sequence of points
(y,y)\ldots(y,N)
on column
y
. For each
y\leq y^{\prime}\leq N
and each

\psi\in TF^{\varphi}_{A}
, the following cases are the only admissible ones in a consistent compass structure:

•

\psi\in\mathrm{Req}_{A}(\mathcal{L}(y,y))
and
\psi
belongs to the atom
\mathcal{L}(y,y^{\prime\prime})
, for some
y\leq y^{\prime\prime}\leq y^{\prime}
. In such a case, there are not pending requests for the suffix

(y,y^{\prime})\ldots(y,N)
(as far as
\psi
is concerned).
We add the symbol
\psi^{\checkmark}
to the labelling of point
(y,y^{\prime})
to record that
\psi
has been fulfilled on column
y
.

•

\psi\in\mathrm{Req}_{A}(\mathcal{L}(y,y))
and
\psi

does not
belong to the atom
\mathcal{L}(y,y^{\prime\prime})
, for all
y\leq y^{\prime\prime}\leq y^{\prime}
. In such a case,
\psi
is not satisfied in the prefix
(y,y)\ldots(y,y^{\prime})
and, in order to guarantee the fulfillment of the
\mathsf{A}
-requests of
(y,y)
we have to impose that
\psi
appears in the labelling of some point in the suffix

(y,y^{\prime}+1)\ldots(y,N)
. We add the symbol
\psi^{\scalebox{0.7}{$\uparrow$}}
to the labelling of point
(y,y^{\prime})
to record that
the fulfillment of
\psi
on the column
y
has been
delegated to the suffix
(y,y^{\prime}+1)\ldots(y,N)
, i.e., the “upward” part of column
y
.

•

\psi\notin\mathrm{Req}_{A}(\mathcal{L}(y,y))
. In such a case, from the consistency of atoms, it follows that
[A]\neg\psi\in\mathcal{L}(y,y)
, and thus
\psi\notin\mathcal{L}(y,y^{\prime\prime})
, for all
y\leq y^{\prime\prime}\leq y^{\prime}
. We add the symbol
\psi^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$\notin$}}}
to the labelling of point
(y,y^{\prime})
to record that
\psi
does not belong to
\mathcal{L}(y,y^{\prime})
because of the constraint
[A]\neg\psi\in\mathcal{L}(y,y)
(in fact, such a constraint must be associated with all points in the sequence
(y,y)\ldots(y,N)
).

It is worth noticing that the above three conditions are mutually exclusive. The next example gives a graphical account of the
behaviour of the labellings
\psi^{\scalebox{0.7}{$\uparrow$}}
,
\psi^{\scalebox{0.7}{$\checkmark$}}
, and

\psi^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$\notin$}}}
.

0

1

2

3

4

5

6

7

\emptyset

\mathrm{Req}_{A}(\mathcal{L}(1,1))=\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\}

\emptyset

\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}\psi_{1}^{\scalebox{0.7}{$\uparrow$}}\ \psi_{2}^{\scalebox{0.7}{$\uparrow$}}\ \psi_{3}^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$\notin$}}}\end{array}\!\!\!

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\psi_{1}}

\mathrm{Req}_{A}(\mathcal{L}(3,3))=\{\psi_{2},\psi_{3}\}

\emptyset

\!\!\!\begin{array}[]{c}{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\psi_{1}^{\scalebox{0.7}{$\checkmark$}}}\\
\psi_{2}^{\scalebox{0.7}{$\uparrow$}}\\
\psi_{3}^{{\scalebox{0.7}{$\notin$}}}\end{array}\!\!\!

\psi_{1}

\mathrm{Req}_{A}(\mathcal{L}(5,5))=\{\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\}

{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\psi_{2}}
